# Design envelope

Margo is a small WebGPU engine: core plus the stage facade is 23,626 B gzip (`npm run measure:size` prints the exact figure; the build fails at 25,000 bytes; core alone is 21,285 B of its 21,587 B cap (2026-11-07: `CADCameraControls` moved to the stage, core 21,586 B to 20,672 B and core + stage 23,013 B to 23,016 B; stable instance rows with changed-row uploads then added 382 B and `Engine.writePoses` 231 B, so core + stage is 23,626 B); the 2026-10-30 core cleanup, which removed the view target depth copy pass, `mesh.euler` and four small duplicates, took core from 21,176 B to 20,790 B and core + stage from 22,580 B to 22,189 B; storing a caller's 4x view target depth then added 23 B to each, a package's frame in one render pass 293 B to core + stage, and `Engine.renderTarget` 44 B), and every
other capability is an optional package. That size is a decision, and so are the boundaries below. Each one exists
for one of two reasons: it keeps core small and frozen, or it follows the limits WebGPU guarantees on every adapter
(Margo's device requests none above them, so an app that runs on a laptop also runs on a phone). This page lists every
hard limit, where it starts, what you see when you reach it, and the package or tool that takes you past it.

How to read "Starts at":

- **Enforced**: an exact constant in the source (linked), usually with the test that asserts it.
- **Measured**: a committed result file (linked). Unless a row says otherwise: ASUS Zenbook S 14, Intel Core Ultra 7
  258V with an Intel Arc 140V integrated GPU, Windows 11, Edge 155, AC power, GPU lock held.
- **Not measured**: the boundary exists, but nobody has run the case. No number is given.

Every number here has a row in [whitepaper/claims.md](whitepaper/claims.md) (LIM-001 to LIM-605). Past losses and their
fixes are in [whitepaper/perf-history.md](whitepaper/perf-history.md).

## Scale

| Limit | Starts at | Why | What you see | Escape hatch |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Entities (core meshes, assembly nodes included) | Enforced: 8,388,608 slots (2^23; [kernel.ts](../packages/core/src/kernel.ts), [core-dirty-transforms.test.js](../tests/core-dirty-transforms.test.js)). The store starts at `capacity` (default 1,024) and doubles on demand. Measured ([results-scale-node.json](../examples/package-benchmarks/parts/results-scale-node.json), Node 24, quiet-gated): 352.1 B of kernel memory per slot at 1,048,576 slots (351 B by layout); a static kernel tick costs 3.3 ms at 100,006 entities, 20.2 ms at 499,992 and 41.5 ms at 1,000,012. Above 1,048,576 slots: not measured. | One float32 structure-of-arrays Wasm kernel computes every transform, bound and visibility. It is the reason core is small and fast, and its per-entity cost is linear: about 41 ns per entity per frame even when nothing moves. | Growth is automatic. Past the cap `createMesh` throws `RangeError: Capacity`; `Engine.create` with a larger `capacity` throws `RangeError: Capacity: 1..8388608`. A growth that cannot allocate throws and leaves the store as it was. Before the cap, frame time grows with entity count. | `@margoengine/culling`. `CulledInstances` keeps parts out of the entity store entirely: 0.41-0.55 ms CPU per frame at 89,883 parts, flat in part count ([results-culling.json](../examples/package-benchmarks/parts/results-culling.json)); parts lose picking, hierarchy and section caps. `CulledMeshes` GPU-culls parts that must stay core meshes; it removes GPU work, not the kernel tick. |
| Nodes and parts per file in packages | Enforced, default 100,000 ([limits.js](../packages/shared/limits.js), [count-limits.test.js](../tests/count-limits.test.js)): glTF nodes ([gltf](../packages/gltf/src/index.ts), [gltf-pro asset.ts](../packages/gltf-pro/src/asset.ts)), `.margo` nodes ([blender](../packages/blender/src/index.ts)). | Input validation that bounds memory for untrusted files: every node or part becomes a core mesh. | `RangeError: Too many glTF nodes (100001 > 100000; raise the maxNodes option)`; likewise `Too many .margo nodes` (`maxNodes`). | The option the error names, up to core's 8,388,608 entities: `maxNodes` on the glTF, gltf-pro and `.margo` loaders; a value outside 1 to 8,388,608 throws `maxNodes must be an integer from 1 to 8388608`. Load time and memory past 100,000: not measured. |
| Buffers per geometry | Enforced: a geometry's vertex, index and instance buffers (96 B per instance) must each fit the device's `maxBufferSize`; core requests the WebGPU default ([renderer.ts](../packages/core/src/renderer.ts)). Not measured. | WebGPU's guaranteed limits, so the same scene runs on every adapter. | `RangeError: GPU buffer limit` when the geometry is first drawn. | Split the geometry, or draw the copies with `CulledInstances`. |
| Instance rows after culling flips (2026-11-07) | By method: a mesh that culling or `visible` shows takes its batch's next row and one hidden gives its row to the batch's last, so row order inside a batch, and with it draw order among one geometry's instances, follows the order of flips. Proved exact as a set, row for row and in the uploaded GPU rows on 200 random frames of flips and moves ([core-instance-cache.test.js](../tests/core-instance-cache.test.js)) | A one-bit change costs one row (2 map lookups, 24 floats, 96 uploaded bytes), not a full collection and a full upload | Two core meshes of one geometry at exactly the same depth may swap which one shows after a flip. Several cameras on one scene flip most rows and cost what a full collection costs. The WebGL2 renderer still uploads every row each frame | Give coincident surfaces a small offset or separate geometries |
| Failed allocations in core (2026-11-07) | By method: `Engine.retain` makes the instance array and the GPU buffers before it changes anything, and `Renderer.add` frees old buffers only after the new ones exist. Covers what throws: the `GPU buffer limit` check, a failed CPU array, `@margoengine/webgl`'s GL errors. Proved on a fake device with the real `Engine` and `GPURenderer` ([deep-core-allocation-failure.test.js](../tests/deep-core-allocation-failure.test.js)), and on a real destroyed device in `npm run test:lifecycle:browser` (LIM-330). A failed `rebootDevice` leaves the engine and the canvas on the device they had: the canvas moves last, proved on a real healthy device in the same suite | While a batch grows it holds both sets of buffers, about 1.5 times the new size at the peak. WebGPU reports a real out-of-memory from `createBuffer` asynchronously, not as a throw | A real out-of-memory shows as a `gpu-lost` error on the next frame, with no rollback | Size geometry to the device's `maxBufferSize`; retry a failed `setGeometry` after freeing memory |
| `Picking.pickMany` points per call | Staging memory: 768 bytes a point, freed when the call ends ([picking.ts](../packages/selection/src/picking.ts)). No enforced count. | One 3 x 3 tile a point at WebGPU's 256-byte row pitch. All points read the scene at the call's turn, where separate `engine.pick` calls each read it at their own. | Memory use during a call grows with the number of points, 768 bytes each, until the call ends. | Split a very large list into several calls. |
| Shader variants per engine | Enforced: 64 cached pipeline variants ([renderer.ts](../packages/core/src/renderer.ts)). How close real scenes come: not measured. | Bounds pipeline compilation and memory. Variants come from MSAA, target format and shader text; each pick shader text (`engine.pick` with the scene's pass hooks) is one more. | `RangeError: Shader limit: 64`. | Not needed so far; fewer distinct shader patches per engine. |
| Biome world streaming (`@margoengine/biomes/view`) | Enforced by the check `packages/biomes/tests/flythrough-check.mjs` (run by `check:merge`): a 300 km/h flight over the zero-config world, tiles made by 4 workers as the camera goes, may add at most 0.3 ms to the slowest 1 percent of frames and 1 ms to the worst frame against the same flight with every tile made first; the page spends at most `budget` (default 0.25 ms) a frame sending jobs and copying tiles in. Tile jobs reuse their working arrays: placing the next tile makes 2.1 MB of fresh arrays (24.0 MB before) and a view tile 0.2 MB (4.6 MB before), and a tile's view and placement jobs share one world tile instead of making it twice (`packages/biomes/tests/tile-memory.test.js`). Measured on the RTX A6000 desktop, Edge 156: p99 +0.20 ms, worst +0.00 ms; 111 tiles needed in a flight, 2 arrive after the camera needs them, tile latency p99 337 to 348 ms. On the coordinator's laptop (Ryzen 7 7735HS with a Radeon 680M, 4 workers), three runs from a cool start at the landed code: p99 +0.1, +0.0 and -0.3 ms, worst +0.3, +0.9 and +0.6 ms (before these fixes: p99 +0.3 to +0.6 ms, worst up to +1.8 ms); its prefetched flights themselves vary from run to run (p50 0.8 to 1.4 ms), so a negative figure is that variation, not a gain. | Workers that threw away megabytes of fresh memory per tile slowed the page's own frames on a laptop APU (Ryzen 7 7735HS with a Radeon 680M: cores share memory and power) and failed the check there (p99 +0.3 to +0.6 ms). The fixes on that laptop: a frame sends the jobs it plans within its budget and a frame that plans leaves sending and copying to the next; a send starts only when the budget left covers what a send that reached a worker took lately (a job that only waits in the pool's queue costs nothing and does not count), while copying goes on while budget is left; terrain tiles go in a band of rows at a time; objects have scatter slots for every tile a view keeps (the laptop once ran out of them: `ScatterTiles has no free slot`). The plan of a still camera comes 30 frames after the last plan, not every 30th frame (it once fell right after a plan of the moving camera, and that second plan streamed too: 1 run in 5 failed the plan-frames check); three cool runs with that fix: p99 +0.1, +0.1 and +0.2 ms, worst +0.8, +0.8 and +0.3 ms. The landed code (b8210cd3), three runs: 2 of 3 pass; run 1 p99 +0.1, worst +0.6 ms; run 2 p99 +0.1, worst +1.0 ms, exactly at the limit; run 3 fails: p99 +0.4 ms against 0.3, worst +0.4 ms; every plan, send and packing line was clean in all three. Counting all runs of this streaming code on that laptop, 8 of 9 pass. The one miss is 0.1 ms (one timer step) over the p99 limit, in a run whose streamed flights had fewer frames (703 to 1,083 against about 1,150 to 1,200). On a 680M integrated GPU the margin is thin and the check can miss by one timer step; on the RTX A6000 it passes with p99 +0.10 to +0.20 ms. The limits were not loosened. Figures are medians of three flights per kind. Every worker still runs at once: a fixed cap of half the workers was tried and dropped, it made tiles late on that laptop and cost fast machines late tiles too. | Each world keeps its tile working arrays and each placement its candidate tables between tiles (about 15 MB per worker at the default 256 m tiles) until disposed. The view entry is 7,514 B gzip, over its earlier 7,374 B quality budget; up to 7,516 B accepted by Scott 2026-10-29. | `workers` on `BiomeWorld.create`; `budget`, `lookahead` and `radius` on `BiomeView.attach`; `view.prefetch(rect)` before a planned flight. |
| Biome world CPU speed (`@margoengine/biomes`, goal G1) | Missed: the goal is 400 ms per km2 on one thread (fields plus placement of R16 at 1 m), and this machine measures fields 376 to 444 ms (best and middle of 30 tiles, RTX A6000 PC with other jobs running) plus placement 177 to 222 ms. Quiet-machine numbers come with the biomes benchmarks. | On 8 workers each replica makes the 9 whole tiles a placed tile reads, so neighbours are made more than once (`bench/biomes/placement.mjs`). What holds it: exact integer fields per cell (CORDIC aspect, Catmull-Rom heights, climate). | Biome world generation runs slower than the 400 ms per km2 goal, as the times in the Starts at cell show. | The GPU path (row 27.4 B8) is the designed route to the goal; the goal is unchanged. |
| Biome objects beyond `radius` (`@margoengine/biomes/view`) | Stored trees, shrubs, rocks and props are drawn only for tiles within `radius` (default 1,000 m) of the camera, and grass and other detail within `detailRadius` (default 120 m). Trees go on to `far.radius` (default 2,500 m) as 12-triangle stand-ins over the world's canopy cover; shrubs, rocks and props stop at `radius`. Measured at the default: the zero-config world holds 162,776 streamed objects in 12.0 MB of GPU memory, 5.9 MB of it records and rings and the rest scatter's cull in candidate order (Rendering, "Scatter cull in candidate order"; `packages/biomes/tests/objects-check.mjs`, RTX A6000, 2026-11-05). Held objects grow with the area inside `radius`, so about four times as many at 2,000 m (expected from the area, not measured). | A stand-in is one coloured solid, hashed over the cover, not the placed tree: impostors or a far LOD of placed records would be a new scatter system, kept out of v1.0.0. | Stand-ins are lit by the engine light and cast no shadow; past `far.radius` far hills keep the darker canopy ground only. | Raise `far.radius` and `far.count`, or `radius` (memory and placement work grow with its square); `far: false` and your own far layer. |
| Biome root entry and GPU backend (`@margoengine/biomes`, accepted difference in size, Scott 2026-11-05) | Measured with `npm run quality -- --gate=sizes` (2026-11-05): root 16,329 B gzip, 553 B over its old 15,776 B budget and 329 B over the spec's 16,000 B; the GPU kernels are the lazy `@margoengine/biomes/gpu` entry, 21,714 B (14,425 B before it also took the flow and placement kernels a backend `'gpu'` world routes `hydrologyOfAsync` and `BiomePlacement` through), which only a world that may compute on a device downloads. Before the kernels moved out the root was 24,637 B. | The root bytes buy backend `'gpu'` and its `device` option (a small hook; checks, bands, device loss and kernels sit in the GPU entry), per-caller tile cancellation (one caller's abort no longer rejects a cached tile other callers wait on), an `update()` that leaves the world unchanged when a change throws, one revision of the world per request across awaited GPU bands, and the crop and pack step the CPU and GPU paths share. About 214 B came earlier from the J5 classify split and field validation. | A CPU world downloads the root only; a GPU world adds the GPU entry once. | `backend: 'cpu'` (or `'auto'` without a device) never loads the GPU entry. |
| Biome worker file (`dist/workers/biomes.js`) | 39,090 B gzip at level 9 and 103,858 B raw (2026-11-08, batch 64; 48,403 B gzip and 129,928 B raw with every optional part inside, commit d04991dda; 51,332 B and 141,164 B before that), against the design's first estimate of about 18 kB. The optional parts are files of their own beside it, each run by a worker with `importScripts` only when its world uses the part, from URLs the page resolves with `new URL(..., import.meta.url)` (so the app's bundler copies them) and sends with each replica's first message: `biomes-splines.js` 4,245 B, `biomes-erosion.js` 2,557 B, `biomes-graph.js` 2,952 B, `biomes-zones.js` 1,790 B and `biomes-multinoise.js` 2,142 B (gzip level 9). The worker build ([build-workers.js](../scripts/build-workers.js)) swaps the GPU, digest and presets entries for an empty stub ([worker-empty-stub.js](../scripts/worker-empty-stub.js)): a worker's world never gets a device, no worker job makes a digest, and the page sends its world's biomes, so the replica never needs the default ones. It resolves the package's own entries to their sources, and a part reads every module it shares with the worker from the worker's copy, so the six files hold one copy of each module (and each hook one object); `packages/biomes/tests/workers.test.js` counts one copy of the integer helpers, each part's code in its own file only and none of the digest or presets code, and measures the worker. Lazy: only a world created with `workers` loads it, and it counts toward no entry's budget. | A worker runs the whole tile path by itself: world, region, relief, placement, cover and the view's tile functions. Reaching about 18 kB would also take placement, the view and cover tile functions out, which every view with objects needs, so the worker would do less of the tile. The worker keeps every export of the modules its parts share, about 940 B gzip over a trial build without the parts (38,153 B). | A world with `workers` downloads the worker file once and each part it uses once; every worker shares the browser's copies. | Leave `workers` off and tiles are made on the page's thread. |
| Biome rivers (`@margoengine/biomes/view`) | Fixed for v1.0.0: river and lake water stands still (`water: { rivers: 'auto' }` or `'hydrology'`, 1.5 m over the bed, falling with it; `lakes: 'auto'` at the hydrology's fill level); it does not flow. Off by default. | A flowing river needs the shallow-water passes to run on steep beds at a cost not yet measured, so it comes after v1.0.0. The view couples at most 512 water cells a side (16 m on an 8 km world). | Valleys are dry unless asked for. A river under two cells wide can break up, and past 0.375 of the world's side from the camera the water grid coarsens. | Attach your own `Water` with `terrain`, `fill` and, for the flow, no `still`. |
| Biome settlements (`@margoengine/biomes/settlements`, 2026-11-08) | Enforced: `villages` and `sites` at most 64, `houses` at most 256 a village, `spacing` 50 to 100,000 m, `cell` 2 to 256 m and at most 4,194,304 grid cells, `maxGrade` 0.02 to 1, `siteGrade` 0.005 to 1, `bridge` 1 to 1,000, `detour` 1 to 100, `yard` 0 to 100 m. Measured (diagnostic, Node on the busy Beelink): about 1.4 s for the default 4 km world at the 16 m cell, three villages (LIM-524). | Roads are least-cost paths on the site grid, one A* search a link, so the layout reads the ground once a cell and its time grows with the cells and the links; a finer `cell` follows the ground more closely at the square of the cost. | Roads bend at grid cells (thinned to 0.75 of a cell, then a Catmull-Rom curve), so on a 16 m cell a road can cut a tight valley's corner by a few metres; houses are rectangles on straight lanes, with no building mesh; a village on hilly relief gets fewer than `houses` lots (each lot must be level within `siteGrade`); rivers count only where `rivers` lines say, and a road never crosses the sea. | Pass `sites` to place villages yourself, a smaller `cell` for closer roads, a larger `siteGrade` for terraced lots, or your own paths to /splines. |
| Biome placement graphs (`@margoengine/biomes/graph`, 2026-11-08) | Enforced: 1 to 256 nodes; ids of letters, digits, `_` and `-`; `multiply`, `min`, `max` and `add` take 2 to 16 inputs; curves 2 to 16 points with x increasing; noise `scale` 1 to 100,000 whole metres; every value 0..1; no cycle; every output selector selects a species of the world. By method: the graph runs once per candidate, before the spacing rounds, so a cost that grows with nodes times candidates. Unmeasured: time per node (LIM-539). | A density factor at each candidate keeps placement's integer decisions and its worker and GPU paths, whose shared CPU tail reads the same factor. | A graph only lowers a species' density (factors are 0..1) and does not read masks, paint, the canopy or other species; detail species (grass, flowers) are not affected; spacing is still set by the species' radii. | Raise a species' `density` and let the graph cut it back; write a node's values with `evaluateGraph` and `world.writeLayer` as a `mask/<name>` for envelopes and paint masks to read. |
| Biome ecosystem growth (`@margoengine/biomes/ecosystem`, 2026-11-08) | Enforced: 0 to 2,000 years; 1 to 1,000,000 plants held (default 200,000; seedlings past it are counted in `crowded`); crowns 0.1 to 40 m; 0 to 64 seedlings per adult a year; dispersal scale 0.5 to 2,000 m, capped at 30 scales; bounds and plants within 100 km of the origin. By method: every year rebuilds a crown grid and tests each plant against the crowns of its 3 x 3 cells, so a cost that grows with plants times neighbours times years. Unmeasured: time per plant-year (LIM-544). | An individual model on integers (centimetres, millimetres, Q16.16) with world-anchored hashes gives the same forest on every machine and lets a run start from a placed tile. | Light is a crown-overlap share, not a ray-cast canopy; no soil, water, climate or seed bank; plants do not move or lean into gaps; the result is a list of plants, not a placement tile. | Feed `where` from a world's fields for site quality; run a smaller plot and repeat it; place the survivors with your own scatter or as founders of another run. |
| Frame allocation (JavaScript heap per steady-state frame) | Measured by the check `npm run test:frame-alloc:browser` ([frame-alloc-browser.mjs](../scripts/frame-alloc-browser.mjs), scenes in [tests/fixtures/frame-alloc](../tests/fixtures/frame-alloc/main.js); run by `check:merge`). RTX A6000 desktop, Edge 156 headless with `--enable-precise-memory-info`: `performance.memory.usedJSHeapSize` read once per frame, 600 warm-up frames (one of them, 60 before recording, late on purpose: a 200 ms stall, so the floors hold for the steady state after a late frame) then 600 recorded, frames with a garbage collection left out, the median frame, largest of three runs, at the item 53.1 commit (`perf(core, shadows, post)`, 2026-10-29). Per frame, before and after (two steps, item 53.1 parts 1 and 2): three lit meshes 1,428 B to 340 B; 10,000 moving instanced boxes 1,076 B to 292 B; the same room with cascaded shadows and eight moving casters 26,920 B to 860 B; with SSAO, Bloom, tone mapping and FXAA 5,116 B to 1,528 B. The room plus 10,000 `DynamicInstances` boxes (@margoengine/culling), every row rewritten each frame through bulk writes and `update()`, measured 396 B in three runs at the item 53.2 commit (56 B over the room alone, among them the 16 B wrapper of its compute pass). The check fails when a median frame passes these floors; they are only ever lowered. | Every byte a frame allocates is a byte the garbage collector must sweep later, and a collection in the middle of a frame is a stutter. Margo reuses its arrays, descriptors and callbacks from frame to frame instead of making new ones. | Not 0 B, and it cannot reach 0 B: every WebGPU call that returns an object (the command encoder and its finished command buffer, each render pass, the canvas's current texture and its view) hands back a new 16 B JavaScript wrapper from the browser, and V8 stores the fractional frame time it passes to frame callbacks and plugins as a heap number. Shadows and post-processing add passes, so they add wrappers. Measured and left as they are: the frame-resources view set clears about 120 B per frame in the post scene (a fix changes the protocol between copies of the resources, judged risky for a frozen core), the SSAO scene walk makes an iterator and an options literal, the fixture setters box about 216 B in the shadowed scene, and each returned WebGPU object costs 16 B. Number boxing in V8's middle tier (Maglev) is gated too: `tests/frame-alloc.test.js` fails a core frame over 780 B, shadows over 620 B and the cascade fit over 128 B there on V8 14 (Node 26) and newer. V8 13 (Node 24, the minimum Node) passes the core limit (628 B) but its Maglev calls Math.sqrt with a boxed argument and result, 32 B a call, so it has its own row for the other two: shadows over 1,120 B (measured 1,081 B, 704 B of it 22 square roots) and the cascade fit over 256 B (measured 242 B, 224 B of it 7 square roots); a square root in JavaScript arithmetic with the same bits would cost every engine more time. Node 22 has no Maglev by default, so the middle-tier probes skip there. A late frame once deoptimized core's frame loop (its delta clamp first ran a compare on a frame past `maxDelta`, with no type feedback), and the loop then ran its next thousands of frames in a tier that boxes numbers: 24 to 84 B more per frame in every scene, seen only when `check:merge` loaded the machine. Fixed in core, and gated twice: the browser check's late warm-up frame, and `tests/frame-alloc.test.js`, which fails when a late frame leaves the loop unoptimized. The Node probes compile on the main thread (`--no-concurrent-recompilation`), so a package build running beside them cannot make them measure a lower tier. `npm run test:frame-alloc:nodes` runs the test on every Node it finds; the probes leave out windows in which V8 collected garbage. In these runs 0 to 3 of 600 recorded frames held a collection. | Keep your own `onFrame` callbacks allocation-free (the check's scenes keep their clock in a `Float64Array`). `node scripts/frame-alloc-browser.mjs --profile --source` names the functions that allocate per frame. |
| Moving transforms per frame (CPU budget) | Measured by [transforms-budget.mjs](../examples/package-benchmarks/transforms-budget.mjs): every mesh sets `position.y` and `rotation.y` through the setters each frame, one shared geometry and material (one instanced draw), all in view, Node v26.12.0 on an AMD Ryzen 9 9950X (32 threads), a stand-in GPU device so the figure is the CPU work only (the onFrame callback, the Wasm kernel tick, instance patching, the renderer's frame). 120 warm-up frames, 300 timed, the median frame, a binary search for the largest count within 4 ms (a quarter of a 60 Hz frame). Result: 14,500 moving transforms per frame (record: [transforms-budget.json](../examples/package-benchmarks/results/transforms-budget.json), item 53.1 part 2 commit). Three runs on a machine busy with other checks gave 14,500, 16,000 and 17,700; the lowest is the figure. | A scene can move this many objects every frame before the engine's own CPU work passes a quarter of a 60 Hz frame. | The count at 4 ms depends on the CPU, the Node and browser build, and on what else is running; it is the engine's CPU work only, not GPU time, culling of hidden objects or your own callbacks. | Move fewer objects per frame, or leave resting objects untouched: only a changed transform is patched. Run the script on your machine. |
| Voxel worlds and render distance | Measured ([voxel-sandbox-bench.json](../examples/package-benchmarks/results/voxel-sandbox-bench.json), RTX A6000 desktop, Edge 155, 1920 × 1080, 4x MSAA, quiet-gated, `examples/voxel-sandbox` seed 1): render distance 8 (1,379 chunks loaded, 602 with quads, 1.18 M quads, 14.5 MB of quads on the GPU, 15.9 MB of chunk data, 322 draws) fills in 1.06-1.22 s with frame CPU 0.7 ms median, 1.1 ms p95; render distance 16 (5,579 loaded, 2,435 with quads, 4.84 M quads, 59.5 MB, 64.1 MB, 1,170 draws) fills in 3.59-3.67 s (8.8-9.2 s before 2026-10-20) with 1.7 ms median, 2.2 ms p95 right after the third fill (1.3 ms median once settled, about 4 s later). While the world fills (the sandbox joins 2 ms of chunks a frame then) frame CPU is 4.2 ms median, 8.8 ms p95 at render distance 8 and 6.6 / 11.1 ms at 16, worst 15.8 ms. Flying at 11 blocks/s while chunks stream in: frame CPU p95 3.7 ms (render distance 8) and 5.0 ms (16), worst frames 6.8 and 10.2 ms; back to back with the code before 2026-10-20 at 16: 4.2 / 8.1 ms before, 3.9 / 7.4 ms after. Voxel GPU time from timestamps between passes is rough: 0.39-2.56 ms median at render distance 8 and 0.72-1.51 ms at 16 over three runs. Laptops and phones: not measured. | Each loaded chunk keeps its block palette and light on the CPU; each chunk with faces keeps 12-byte quads in one GPU pool (`maxQuadBytes`, default 96 MiB); generation and meshing run in workers, joining and light on the main thread spread over frames (`chunksPerFrame`, `frameBudget`); chunks above a column's ceiling, when the generator gives one, load as air without a job. | Filling the range takes time in proportion to the chunks in it, about 1,550 chunks a second here with a 2 ms join budget (about 680 with the default 0.5 ms, which keeps frames light during play). A full quad pool leaves far chunks unmeshed until near ones free pages. Worlds wider than float32 precision (about 100 km) jitter: voxels have no floating origin in v1 (packages/voxel/DESIGN.md, decision 4). | A smaller `distance` on `VoxelStreamer`; a larger `frameBudget` and `chunksPerFrame` while a loading screen shows; a larger `maxQuadBytes` (up to the device's storage binding limit); more `workers` for the mesher and streamer. |
| Voxel slabs and stairs | Measured (Node on the RTX A6000 desktop, `npm run bench -- voxel`): meshing the 128-chunk bench terrain takes 85.5-86.1 ms with slabs and stairs on two thirds of its surface (`mesh-128-shaped-chunks`; 78.2-79.2 ms before waterlogging, same quads and bytes) against 39.8-41.0 ms without them (the same as before slabs existed, compared in one process; 106,971 quads), and 99.2 ms with water, double slabs, waterlogged slabs and stairs and corner stairs there (`mesh-128-mixed-shape-chunks`). | Their faces, cube faces and liquid faces beside them are meshed per quarter face with half-block AO and light; a chunk with solid ones gets one more physics triangle mesh. | Half-block shapes only: no slopes, no slabs of two materials in one cell (a double slab is one block id), and a waterlogged cell holds only its type's `waterlog` liquid as a full source (no flowing level). VOX files have no shapes, so `.vox` imports are whole blocks. | Whole blocks where shapes are not needed; leave `TraceModel.shapes` out to trace whole cells. |
| Far voxel levels (`@margoengine/voxel/lod`, 2026-11-07) | Enforced: 1 to 12 levels, `radius` 2 to 32 nodes, `scale` a power of two to 4,096. By method: a level-L cell is 2^L blocks, so detail under a cell is gone (a tree, a one-block wall) and a coarse surface is within half a cell of the real one; `noiseTerrainLod` leaves out caves and trees; edits to the real world show in far levels only where its chunks are loaded; nodes generate on the main thread, `budget` a frame; the streamed chunks must stay inside level 1 (`2 * radius` above the streamer's `distance` + 1) or the two overlap; while a level changes, old and new nodes may both show for a few frames; a scaled renderer takes no lighting effects, shadows or capture. Frame cost at kilometre views is unmeasured. | Each far level is a coarser copy of the world, so it cannot hold detail finer than its cells. | Trees and one-block walls are gone in far levels, and old and new nodes may both show for a few frames while a level changes. | Pass your own `generate` function (`VoxelLodOptions.generate`) to put trees or walls into far cells; `noiseTerrainLod` is only one generator and leaves them out. |
| Voxel light in a worker (`@margoengine/voxel/light-worker`, 2026-11-07) | By method: the worker holds a second copy of every loaded chunk's blocks and light; the page still packs each loaded chunk once and copies the light of changed chunks back (32 KiB each); one worker per world, not with `VoxelLighting` on the same world. | Light is worked out in a worker on its own copy of the chunks. | Light arrives a frame or more after a change. | Use `VoxelLighting.attach(world)` on the page instead: it keeps no second copy of the chunks, but its flood fill runs on the page thread. |
| Coloured block light (`@margoengine/voxel/light-rgb`, 2026-11-07) | Enforced: colours 0..1, `extent` multiples of 32 up to 1,024. By method: three copies of every loaded chunk (blocks and light) and three floods per change; coloured light only inside the box around the camera (default 128 x 64 x 128 blocks); not on scaled renderers. | Coloured light keeps three copies of every loaded chunk and floods three times per change, so it is limited to a box around the camera. | A level is rounded per channel, so dim colours lose hue. | The renderer's own block light should be switched off (`blockLightColor` [0, 0, 0]) or it adds on top. |
| Flowing liquids (`@margoengine/voxel/fluids`, 2026-11-07) | Enforced: `reach` 1 to 7, `delay` 1 or more, at most `maxUpdates` cells a tick and 10 ticks an update. | By method: levels in eighths (state data), so a liquid runs at most 7 cells from a source; | flow toward drops looks 4 cells ahead; no currents pushing bodies, no pressure (a liquid never rises); unloaded chunks stop it like rock; speed on large pools unmeasured. | Place further sources to carry a liquid past 7 cells (`infinite: true` makes two neighbouring sources renew one); `maxUpdates` caps the cells updated a tick on large pools. |
| Block ticks (`@margoengine/voxel/ticks`, 2026-11-07) | Enforced: `randomTicks` 0 to 4,096 a chunk, at most `maxUpdates` scheduled and neighbour updates a tick, 10 ticks an update. | By method: changes made by hooks run next tick (no recursion), so a chain of falling blocks moves one cell per tick plus its delay; random ticks cost every loaded chunk every tick; falling blocks are cells, not bodies. | A chain of falling blocks moves one cell per tick plus its delay, and random ticks cost every loaded chunk every tick. | Lower `randomTicks` (default 24 a chunk, 0 to 4,096) to cut random tick cost, and pass a smaller `delay` to `fallingRule(delay)` (default 2, 1 or more) to speed a falling chain. |
| Voxel GPU meshing (`@margoengine/voxel/gpu-mesher`, 2026-11-08) | Enforced: `batch` 1 to 32 chunks and within the device's storage binding limit (about 3.2 MB of scratch per chunk). By method: chunks with a slab or stair within a cell are meshed on the page; the page still packs each chunk and its shell and flood-fills visibility; two GPU round trips per batch; each slice's greedy merge runs in one invocation, so the GPU path is not faster than `meshChunk` on the M6 Mac mini; one block table per batch. | Chunks with a slab or stair within a cell are meshed on the page, and each slice's greedy merge runs in one invocation. | The GPU path is not faster than `meshChunk` on the M6 Mac mini. | Leave out the `mesher` option of `VoxelRenderer.attach`: its default worker pool meshes with `meshChunk`, which is no slower here. |
| Smooth voxel terrain (`@margoengine/voxel/smooth`, 2026-11-08) | Enforced: densities clamped to -1..1 in 1/127ths, materials 0 to 255, sculpt radius up to 64 and strength -2 to 2, chunk y within `minChunkY` to `maxChunkY`. By method: meshing runs on the page thread (`budget` chunks a frame); no relaxation pass, so the surface keeps a slight cell pattern; one core mesh per chunk and material, plain colour materials (no textures or triplanar mapping); no far levels; past an unloaded neighbour the surface is left open; smooth terrain has no collision with `@margoengine/voxel/physics`. | Meshing runs on the page thread, one core mesh is made per chunk and material, and the surface has no relaxation pass. | A slight cell pattern on the surface, an open surface past an unloaded neighbour, and no collision with `@margoengine/voxel/physics`. | Set `budget` on `SmoothTerrainView` (default 4 chunks an `update()`) to spread meshing over frames, and load the neighbouring chunks to close the surface; `meshSmoothChunk` returns each chunk's positions and indices if you build a collider of your own. |
| Voxel bounce light (`@margoengine/voxel/gi`, 2026-11-08) | Enforced: `strength` 0 to 8, `softness` 0 to 0.5 rad, `cells` 4,096 to 262,144, `rays` 1 to 16, `range` 4 to 64. By method: a 64 x 64 x 64 cell window around the camera; one bounce of sunlight (no sky, block light or multi-bounce); only opaque cubes stop rays and bounce (slabs, stairs, leaves, glass and liquids pass light); light per cell, L1 spherical harmonics, so detail finer than a cell and sharp shadow edges are lost; light settles over several updates after a change; noisy with few `rays`; not on scaled renderers. | Light is kept per cell in a 64 x 64 x 64 window around the camera, as L1 spherical harmonics, and each update traces a few rays. | Detail finer than a cell and sharp shadow edges are lost, light settles over several updates after a change, and few `rays` look noisy. | Raise `rays` for less noise. |
| Voxel destruction (`@margoengine/voxel/debris`, 2026-11-08) | Enforced: `maxCells` 1 to 65,536 (default 4,096: a bigger joined group stays as if held), `maxPieces` 1 or more (default 64), `lifetime` > 0 s, `density` > 0. By method: only removals of solid blocks start a search, on the page thread at `update()`; an unloaded chunk counts as holding; pieces are whole cubes for collider and mesh (slabs and stairs too), flat colour without the world's textures or light, and never go back into the world; non-solid blocks resting on a piece (flowers) stay where they were. | The search after an edit stops at a holding cell or `maxCells`, and a piece is built from whole cells in flat colour so it costs one body and one mesh per block type. | A loose group of `maxCells` blocks or more stays hanging, an overhang into an unloaded chunk stays up, and a falling slab looks like a full block without its texture. | Raise `maxCells` (up to 65,536) for larger pieces, load the neighbouring chunks before cutting, and pass `material` for your own look. |
| Voxel-edit shader brushes (`@margoengine/voxel-edit/shaders`, 2026-11-08) | Enforced: 1 to 1,024 passes, up to 16 params, a 256-entry table of 0 to 255, values clamped to 255, a grid (and tool) within the device's storage binding limit (128 MiB by default) and `maxCells` (default 16,777,216). By method: every cell of a pass reads the grid as it was before that pass; one submit per pass; the whole grid is uploaded and read back per `run`; brushes see one grid and one tool grid. | Grids are packed four cells to a word in one storage binding, and the CPU builds the selection mask and counts changed cells. | A grid larger than the binding limit is refused, and each `run` pays an upload and a read-back. | Split a large model into tiles with `voxTiles` (`./vox`) and brush each. |
| Voxel-edit rigs (`@margoengine/voxel-edit/rig`, 2026-11-08) | Enforced: unit quaternions (length within 0.001 of 1), unique bone names with parents listed first, parts on known bones, a baked box within `maxCells` (default 16,777,216). By method: each part follows one bone rigidly; a bake gives each cell the part cell under its centre, later parts over earlier ones. | Blocky characters move whole parts; blending cells between bones would tear the grid. | Turns other than quarter turns resample, so a turned part keeps its cell count only roughly; parts do not bend. | Use quarter turns for exact frames; for smooth skinning mesh the parts and use `@margoengine/rigging` with `@margoengine/animation`. |
| Voxel-edit GPU bulk edits (`@margoengine/voxel-edit/gpu`, 2026-11-08) | Enforced: a grid within the device's storage binding limit (128 MiB by default) and `maxCells` (default 16,777,216, at most 67,108,864); fill radius 0 to 128; 0 to 1,024 iterations per filter; a 256-entry remap table of 0 to 255; a brush made on the grid's own device. By method: every edit reads the grid as the edit before left it; one submit per pass; a combine uploads its tool grid each time and counts clipped cells on the CPU. | Exact integer cell work keeps every result equal to the CPU operation. | A grid larger than one storage binding is refused, not split. | Split a large model with `voxTiles` (`./vox`) and edit each tile. |
| Voxel-edit shared editing (`@margoengine/voxel-edit/collab`, 2026-11-08) | Enforced: a site name of 1 to 256 characters, 65,535 sites per replica, clock and seq 1 to 4,294,967,295, cells inside the grid, values 0 to 255, `maxCells` (default 16,777,216). By method: each cell keeps the write with the highest Lamport clock, the higher site name breaking a tie; an operation already applied is ignored. | Last writer wins per cell needs no server and no conflict step, and merges in any order. | Each cell keeps one value, so two users drawing over the same cells keep only the later stroke; a replica keeps 7 bytes a cell plus every operation seen (5 bytes per edited cell), with no compaction. | Start a new shared grid from a snapshot when the log grows too large; give each user a stable site name. |
| Goxel and VoxEdit files (`@margoengine/voxel-edit/formats`, 2026-11-08) | Enforced: `.gox` versions 1 and 2 read, 2 written; BL16 blocks of exactly 64 x 64 pixels; at most 4,096 layers; `maxCells` (default 16,777,216) counts each layer's block bounds before cropping; names and dictionary values written up to 255 bytes. `.vxm` versions 3 to 13 read, 12 written; at most 255 materials (palette indices 1 to 255) and 255 models, all of one size; textures up to 2,048 and face lists up to 262,144 quads are skipped, larger ones refused. By method: from a clean-room specification, not from either program. | Neither format has a public specification; the one Margo reads was written from two programs' behaviour and eight sample files, and lists what it could not determine. | Skipped and lost on a round trip: `.gox` cameras, light, preview, material lists beyond one default on writing, layer matrices of plain layers (written as identity), and the fork-only PLAC, CLRH and CUST chunks; `.vxm` baked meshes, textures, surface data, palette chunks and the trailer. Unknown and so not checked: the `.gox` chunk CRC (no writer computes one; written as 0), the colour space of block colours, the meaning of alpha 1 to 254 (read as solid), clone layers under a rotating `mat` (each voxel centre goes to the cell it lands in), and the inside test at a shape's exact edge (centres on the edge count as inside); `.vxm` versions other than 4 and 12 (no sample files; version 13 in particular rests on one program's reader), the v6 to v10 texture compression, the pivot X convention of v10 and older (returned as stored), and whether VoxEdit opens a file with no baked mesh, as `writeVXM` writes. | Open a written `.vxm` in VoxEdit before relying on it; keep `.vox` (`./vox`) as the exchange file when a model must keep cameras, materials or meshes. |
| Voxel-net entities (`@margoengine/voxel-net/entities`, 2026-11-08) | Enforced: `maxEntities` (default 4,096, at most 1,048,576), `maxPerPlayer` (default 256, nearest first), kind 0 to 65,535, state 0 to 255, `leaveRadius` at least `radius` (default 64 and 10% more). By method: entities over a chunk the server has not loaded do not move; each entity is quantized once per tick and sent as a delta against the player's last message; a player away (parked) gets every entity again in full. | Sending only what is near and changed keeps a crowded world within the 20 Hz tick and each link. | A world of many entities steps every one each tick on the server's thread, with no sleeping of far ones beyond unloaded chunks; brains run on the main thread; no pathfinding (only `wander`). | Keep entities near players; write your own brain for chase or flee; raise `maxPerPlayer` with care for the link. |
| Voxel-net shards (`@margoengine/voxel-net/shards`, 2026-11-08) | Enforced: `regionSize` a multiple of 32 blocks (default 256), `margin` 0 to half the region (default 8), `secret` at least 16 characters, tickets good for `ticketLife` (default 10,000 ms) and once, a handed-off player closed with 4001 after `grace` (default 5,000 ms). Measured on loopback in one process only (two servers, real sockets); not measured between machines. | Each shard is a plain VoxelServer that owns its regions' edits; tickets carry the player across, so no shard needs another's state. | A shard sees only its own edits: the other side of a border shows as generated until the player crosses. A handoff keeps position, not velocity, and lands on the ground; the next shard sends the chunks around the player again. | Put busy areas inside one region; make regions larger than the view distance so borders are crossed rarely. |
| Voxel-net WebTransport (`@margoengine/voxel-net/webtransport`, 2026-11-08) | Enforced: `inputCopies` 0 to 8 (default 3), the copies of one message at most 120 steps; a gap of more than 240 lost steps closes the client with 1002. Measured over an in-memory stand-in link only (a third of datagrams lost each way, every 5th survivor late): no step lost with 3 copies, client and server within 0.05 blocks. | Inputs are small, so copies cost little and make a lost datagram free; whole snapshots cost more bytes than deltas but need no acknowledgement. | A dropped session is not resumed. The slow-client check does not see a WebTransport stream backing up. Players snapshots go whole, so they are larger than over a WebSocket. | Connect again on close; keep `playerDistance` small for crowded worlds; use a WebSocket where WebTransport is missing. |

## Rendering

| Limit | Starts at | Why | What you see | Escape hatch |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| One directional light in core | Enforced by design: core's lighting is one direction and one radiance, plus a colourless scalar ambient. | Every light model beyond one sun is an optional package, so apps that need one sun pay for one sun. | No API for a second sun in core; core's own ambient does not light metals. | `@margoengine/clustered`: up to 1,024 point and spot lights (`RangeError: Expected at most 1,024 lights` past that). `@margoengine/clustered/shadows`: shadows for up to 16 of them ([local-shadows-gpu.json](../examples/package-benchmarks/results/local-shadows-gpu.json)). `@margoengine/clustered/area` (8,946 B gzip): up to 64 rectangle area lights (`RangeError: Expected at most 64 area lights`), unshadowed, no texture bindings ([area-lights-gpu.json](../examples/package-benchmarks/results/area-lights-gpu.json)). More directional lights: `@margoengine/clustered/directional` (1,563 B gzip), up to 16 (`RangeError: Expected an array of at most 16 directional lights`), unshadowed. IES profiles: `@margoengine/clustered/ies` (3,697 B gzip), at most 16 distinct profiles, type C files only. Projector spots: `@margoengine/clustered/projector` (2,618 B gzip), at most 16 images of up to 4,096 texels a side. Physical units: `@margoengine/clustered/units` (833 B gzip). All three light entries use storage buffers, no texture bindings ([light-features-gpu.json](../examples/package-benchmarks/results/light-features-gpu.json)). Configurable ambient: `@margoengine/environment` `HemisphereLight` and `AmbientLight`, one per engine. Image-based lighting: `@margoengine/environment`. |
| 16 sampled textures and 16 samplers per shader stage | Enforced by WebGPU's guaranteed minimum; Margo's device asks for no more. gltf-pro's physical material with directional shadows, IBL and local shadows uses exactly 16 (asserted in [instancing.test.js](../packages/gltf-pro/tests/instancing.test.js)). | Every adapter, phones included, supports 16. Requiring more would make the same app fail on some devices. | A `RangeError` naming the combination, e.g. `gltf-pro: physical material + shadows + environment:lighting + clustered + clustered:shadows + shader patch needs 17 sampled textures per shader stage; this device allows 16`. | Drop one lighting feature for that material, or use the standard material (8 of 16 with everything attached). `VolumetricFog`, SSAO, clustered lights, area lights and light probes (`@margoengine/environment/probes`: one storage buffer; with an environment fallback they take EnvironmentLighting's place and its bindings) add no textures. Texture arrays are planned after 1.0. |
| float32 coordinates | Measured on the GPU ([large-coordinates-gpu.json](../examples/package-benchmarks/results/large-coordinates-gpu.json), 10 mm part, camera 50 mm away): core alone jitters 0.38 px at 1 km and 42 px at 100 km; at 6,000 km the part is not drawn. Per-stage CPU breakdown in [large-coordinates.md](large-coordinates.md). | Core and its kernel store positions and matrices in float32: half the memory and bandwidth of float64, and the format GPUs consume. | No error. Parts shake while the camera moves, small features collapse, specular shifts (2.8° at 100 km), and far enough out geometry disappears. | `FloatingOrigin` in `@margoengine/cameras/origin` (1,258 B gzip, 0 core bytes): 0.019 px jitter at every distance up to 6,000 km, and `engine.pick` hits the part at every distance ([large-coordinates.md](large-coordinates.md#the-plugin)). Vertices or child offsets that are already kilometres from their root inside one asset must be re-centred at export. |
| Transparency | Default: transparent materials and gltf-pro `BLEND` primitives are sorted back to front per mesh; core meshes are opaque. Measured ([oit-gpu.json](../examples/package-benchmarks/results/oit-gpu.json), a 50 % housing over gears orbited 360 × 1°): 13 steps with more than 1 % of covered pixels popping. | Sorting is exact for separate objects and needs no extra targets. | Popping where transparent parts interpenetrate or nest, as the camera moves. | `@margoengine/oit` (2,210 B gzip): 0 popping steps on the same orbit. Weighted blended OIT is an approximation: colour error against exact blending 0.5 / 4.6 / 8.7 (0-255) for 1 / 2 / 4 layers. Transmission and custom blending keep the sorted path unless `blending` is set; limits in its [README](../packages/oit/README.md#limits-the-wboit-approximation). |
| Screen-space reflections | Enforced by method: only what is on screen reflects. Core meshes and models passed to `ssr.add(model)` (gltf-pro, materials, terrain, water) show reflections; other custom shaders are reflected but do not reflect. Cost measured on a worst-case glossy floor ([ssr-gpu.json](../examples/package-benchmarks/results/ssr-gpu.json), quiet-gated): 2.2-2.3 ms at 1280×720, 5.8-5.9 ms at 1920×1080. | Reusing depth and colour that are already rendered avoids a second scene pass. Each custom pipeline needs its own capture into SSR's targets. | Reflections fade at screen edges and miss off-screen objects (reflection probes fill in where attached); water and terrain not added keep their own sky reflection. | `@margoengine/post/ssr` (9,068 B gzip) is itself the opt-in. Off-screen reflections: reflection probes in `@margoengine/environment/probes`; SSR removes the probes' own specular only where its ray hits on screen and keeps the probe's reflection elsewhere ([probes-gpu.json](../examples/package-benchmarks/results/probes-gpu.json): off screen the floor equals the frame without SSR, 95th percentile 0/255). Limits in the [post README](../packages/post/README.md). |
| Volumetric fog | Enforced by design: one froxel grid (default 160 × 90 × 64) whatever the screen size, so shadow edges in the fog are about one froxel wide (8 × 8 px at 1280 × 720). Effects measured in [volumetric-gpu.json](../examples/package-benchmarks/results/volumetric-gpu.json). | A froxel volume makes light shafts follow real shadows at a fixed cost per frame. | Particles, VFX, splats and transparent surfaces are fogged only when added with `fog.add` (additive, multiply and transmission surfaces never); nothing beyond `range`; local fog volumes only from `/fog-volumes`; no multiple scattering. Errors: `RangeError: Volumetric grid exceeds device limit`; `VolumetricFog replaces environment Fog '<name>': set its enabled = false or remove it`; `VolumetricFog needs scene depth: attach a post capture (Tonemap, Bloom, PostStack, SSAO, TAA) first or pass options.depth`. | `@margoengine/environment/volumetric` (6,923 B gzip) is itself the opt-in: a finer `grid` sharpens edges at a higher cost; `fog.texture` lets custom shaders (particles) sample the fog. Limits in the [environment README](../packages/environment/README.md). |
| Light probes (indirect light) | Enforced ([probes.ts](../packages/environment/src/probes.ts), [probes.test.js](../packages/environment/tests/probes.test.js)): one irradiance volume of at most 4,096 L1/L2 probes (64 per axis) and at most 16 box-projected reflection probes per `LightProbes`. The volume is baked; reflection probes are baked or captured live a few cube faces per frame (`setLive`; a moved box shows in a 128 px probe 7-11 frames later at one face per frame, capture GPU time per frame 0.066 ms or less and a median 0.20 ms on the prefilter frame, RTX A6000, [probes-live-gpu.json](../examples/package-benchmarks/results/probes-live-gpu.json)). Measured bake of 8 volume probes and one 128 px reflection probe 114-121 ms (diagnostic: other jobs running; [probes-gpu.json](../examples/package-benchmarks/results/probes-gpu.json)). Accuracy in the same file: volume SH within 1.7% of DC against an analytic room. | Baking turns the scene's light into lookups (no ray tracing per frame) and keeps them in one storage buffer, so no texture binding is spent. | Volume light stays as baked until `bake()` runs again; live reflection probes lag the scene by up to (probes + 1) refreshes of 6 / `facesPerFrame` frames and see only what the frame draws (render with `culling: false`); light leaks through walls thinner than the probe spacing (no visibility per probe); mirror-sharp reflections show texel-sized steps near wall edges at the default 128 px. Errors: `RangeError: Invalid irradiance volume: min < max, 1-64 probes per axis (4,096 in total), sh 1 or 2, blend >= 0`, `RangeError: Expected at most 16 reflection probes`, `RangeError: Light probe buffer exceeds device limit`, `light-probes replaces EnvironmentLighting: pass {environment} to LightProbes instead of attaching both`. | `@margoengine/environment/probes` (14,984 B gzip) is itself the opt-in: re-bake on demand or capture reflections live, one volume box per room against leaks, a finer `resolution` for sharper reflections. Dynamic GI: none yet. |
| Ambient diffuse under `KHR_materials_specular` | Enforced by method ([ibl.ts](../packages/environment/src/ibl.ts)): direct light and `Transmission` weight the base by one number, `1 - max(F)`, as the spec writes it. The split-sum ambient hooks (`EnvironmentLighting`, `LightProbes`, sky) keep what the specular term leaves per channel, so a coloured `specularColorFactor` tints their diffuse slightly. | The spec's scalar would change partly metallic texels of every plain material. | A faint complement tint on diffuse light from an environment, only with a coloured specular. | Light the surface directly, or use a grey specular colour. |
| Environment blend (`@margoengine/environment/blend`, 2026-11-07) | By method: a linear crossfade of two environments' radiance, `(1 - t) x from + t x to`, in the source cube, every prefiltered specular mip and the SH; on an Apple M6 (Metal) every texel of every mip equals the weighted sources within 3 x 2^-11 relative (rgba16float rounding of two inputs and the output; worst seen 9.5e-4) and a quarter-turned source lands texel for texel (LIM-493). Each source's `rotation` and `intensity` are baked in at each `update()`. | Prefiltering and SH projection are linear, so blending their results equals processing the blended image without prefiltering again on each change. | The output holds the memory of a third environment (its own source and specular cubes); the background shows the blended cube, not a source's sharper equirect; changing a source's rotation or intensity needs `update()`; not a time-of-day model. Cost not timed. | Use `@margoengine/sky` for a moving sun; call `update()` after changing a source. |
| Ground projected environment (`@margoengine/environment/ground`, 2026-11-08) | By method: per pixel the view ray meets the floor plane at `center`'s height or the dome (a sphere of `radius`), and the environment is read along the direction from the photo camera (`height` above `center`) to that point; on an Apple M6 (Metal) every pixel from two cameras equals a CPU mirror of that geometry within 0.15% and a floor point seen from the second camera keeps its colour within 0.13% (a plain background: over 3x) (LIM-498). `height` above 0 and below `radius`, `radius` finite, `center` three finite numbers. | Exact analytic geometry in the background's fragment shader; no extra pass. | The floor is flat and the dome a sphere, so walls and objects in the photo bend as the camera moves; the camera must stay inside the dome and above the floor; no contact shadow; cost not timed. | Pick `height` as the photo's tripod height; add `@margoengine/shadows` for contact shadows. |
| Dynamic global illumination (`DynamicGI` in `@margoengine/environment/gi`, 2026-11-08) | Enforced: 1 to 64 probes per axis and at most 4,096, `probesPerFrame` 1 to 4,096, `resolution` a power of two 8 to 64. By method: one sweep of the volume every `ceil(probes / probesPerFrame)` frames, one more bounce per sweep; on an Apple M6 (Metal) a sunlit red wall's red bounce at a point in front of it falls from 0.51 to 0.09 (irradiance / PI) within one sweep after the sun moves behind the wall ((LIM-504)). | Re-capturing a few probes a frame spreads the cost of an irradiance volume bake over its sweep. | A change takes up to one sweep to show; light between probe centres is interpolated, so it leaks through walls thinner than the grid; no specular bounce; captures draw what the frame draws; cost not timed. | More `probesPerFrame` for faster response; a finer grid near thin walls; render with `culling: false`. |
| Probe visibility (`volume.visibility` in `@margoengine/environment/probes`, 2026-11-08) | By method: 24 direction bins per probe (cube face by quadrant) of mean and mean square distance, capped at 1.5 cell diagonals; Chebyshev visibility cubed and a smooth backface term per probe, renormalised. On an Apple M6 (Metal) the light leaking through a thin wall onto a dark floor falls from 0.092 to 0.00004 (irradiance / PI) and the on-screen pixel from 49 to 0 (LIM-543). | Coarse bins keep it to 192 B and one compute pass per captured face. | A bin averages a quarter face, so a wall that covers part of a bin only partly blocks it; a receiver whose eight probes are all occluded takes them equally; walls must be thicker than nothing at the probe's resolution; cost not timed. | Put probes on both sides of every wall; raise `resolution` for thin openings. |
| Sphere and blended reflection probes (`shape: 'sphere'`, `reflectionBlend: 'weighted'` in `@margoengine/environment/probes`, 2026-11-08) | By method: a sphere probe's parallax point is the far hit of the reflected ray with its sphere and its fade runs past the surface; weighted blending sums probes by fade weight, normalised past 1; on an Apple M6 (Metal) both match a CPU mirror within 3.2e-7 at 512 random rays (LIM-549). | No cost without them (box probes in order keep their shader text); a sphere adds one ray-sphere solve per probe per fragment. | Spheres are centred where they are created; capture still sees through each probe's own position only; cost not timed. | Use spheres for round or open areas and weighted blending where probes overlap at a doorway or a clearing. |
| Local fog volumes (`@margoengine/environment/fog-volumes`, 2026-11-08) | Enforced: at most 256 volumes per `FogVolumes`, axis-aligned boxes (`min` < `max`) and spheres (`radius` > 0), `density` 0 to 1,000 per m, `noise` 0 to 1. By method: each froxel adds every volume's extinction and albedo-weighted scattering to the fog's; on an Apple M6 (Metal) the integrated volume of a box, a sphere and a noisy box equals a CPU mirror of the froxel pass within 0.002 + 1% (LIM-545). | The volumes share the fog's froxel grid, so they get its lighting, shafts, shadows and reprojection for one buffer read per froxel and volume. | Every froxel tests every volume, so the froxel pass grows with the count (cost not timed); a volume thinner than a froxel is blurred over it and its neighbours; boxes do not rotate; a volume cannot remove global fog. | Keep the count small and the volumes larger than a few froxels; raise `grid` for small volumes. |
| Procedural studio environment (`@margoengine/environment/studio`, 2026-11-08) | Enforced: `width` a power of two 16 to 4,096 (height half), colours three finite values of 0 or more, per light an angular `width` and `height` in (0, PI), `intensity` 0 to 65,504, `color` 0 to 1 and `softness` 0 to 1. By method: the room depends on elevation alone and a softbox is a gnomonic rectangle with a smoothstep edge; on an Apple M6 (Metal) an Environment made from it keeps the image in its cube within 0.15% and its SH within 0.024% (LIM-547). | No download and deterministic bytes: the same options give the same image on every machine. | An image, not a scene: softboxes are infinitely far (no parallax or distance falloff) and give no contact shadow; made on the CPU in one pass over its texels (8.4 M at width 4,096); cost not timed. | Keep the default width 512 for lighting; add `@margoengine/shadows` for contact shadows. |
| Fogged transparents (`VolumetricFog.add`, `@margoengine/environment/volumetric`, 2026-11-08) | By method: a fragment of an added drawable reads the integrated volume at its own depth and is drawn as straight alpha `a (c T + L) + (1 - a) dst` over the fogged frame, exactly the fogged blend; on an Apple M6 (Metal) the hook equals a CPU mirror within 1.7e-7 (checked under 1e-3) at 192 froxel centres (LIM-548). | One extra pass over the frame for everything added; no shader or call changes without `add`. | Additive, multiply and transmission surfaces keep their own draw unfogged; the fog's lookup is one froxel wide like its composite; cost not timed. | Use normal blending for fogged glass and smoke. |
| Raw `shader` lighting effects on coated materials | Enforced by method ([pbr-hooks.js](../packages/shared/pbr-hooks.js)): only a `pbr` `direct` hook reaches clearcoat and sheen sun lobes. An effect that edits the `direct+ambient(s)` text itself reaches the base's light alone. | The coat's light adds after the sum, unscaled by the share it leaves the layers under it. | Such an effect leaves a coat highlight in its shadow. `Shadows`, `Clustered` and the other effects in this repository use `pbr`. | Return `direct` from `pbr(group)`. |
| Native glass tint draw | Enforced by method ([shader.ts](../packages/gltf-pro/src/shader.ts)): a loaded transmissive material draws twice, a multiply of the light behind and the lit surface, and both sample the material's maps. Cost measured (quiet-gated, RTX A6000, 1920 × 1080 4x, screen-filling glass, [tint-pass-gpu.json](../examples/package-benchmarks/results/tint-pass-gpu.json)): four maps, one layer 0.52 ms a frame against 0.33 ms with no tint draw and 0.39 ms with the older factors-only draw; 16 layers 4.46, 2.56 and 3.47 ms; eight maps, 16 layers 6.88, 3.41 and 4.65 ms. | Base colour, transmission, metal, sheen and clearcoat maps change how much light passes through, so the multiply has to read them. | The tint draw takes 17 to 57 percent of the GPU time of these glass-only frames at 1920 × 1080, 0.03 to 0.2 ms per million glass pixels. Glass with no maps pays as well: 3.41 ms against 2.69 ms for the factors-only draw at 16 layers, because the surface read also covers metal and thin film. | The `Transmission` pass from `@margoengine/materials`. |
| Native glass on a display-encoded target | Open defect ([known-bugs.md](known-bugs.md#native-glass-on-a-display-encoded-target-adds-its-lit-draw-in-encoded-values)): with no HDR scene target. | The blend unit reads the target as stored (plain `bgra8unorm` or `rgba8unorm`), so no blend state can decode it; fixing it needs a scene copy before every glass draw or a core change. | Bright highlights over a bright background come out too bright: encoded 0.5 + 0.5 reads 1.0 where linear light reads 0.69. | `Tonemap`, any PostStack or `install(model)` give the exact sum. |
| Materials surfaces outside the colour pass | Enforced ([cutout-passes-browser.mjs](../packages/materials/test/cutout-passes-browser.mjs), [cutout-casters-browser.mjs](../packages/shadows/test/cutout-casters-browser.mjs)): shadow maps, contact shadows, TAA velocity and picking follow `map` alpha, `alphaMap`, UV transforms, `alphaTest`, `vertexColors: false`, `displacementMap` and `clippingPlanes`. Three limits: `triplanar` keeps its whole surface in those passes; a `static` `ContactShadows` redraws every frame while it has an `alphaMap`, displaced or clipped caster, which change on the GPU alone; and TAA gives a displacement that changes between frames no velocity of its own. Cut-out shadow outlines are also held against Blender Cycles on real glTF models ([check.mjs](../packages/shadows/test/outside/check.mjs)). | Triplanar projects in world space, which a caster's UVs cannot follow; an `alphaMap`, displacement or clipping can change on the GPU alone, so the CPU cannot tell when a static caster is stale, and TAA has no velocity for a displacement that changes between frames. | A `triplanar` surface keeps its whole surface in those passes, a `static` `ContactShadows` redraws every frame in the cases named, and TAA has no velocity for a changing displacement. | Use a UV-mapped `map` instead of `triplanar` where the shape must cut in shadows, picking and TAA; give a `static` `ContactShadows` casters with no `alphaMap`, displacement or clipping so it does not redraw every frame. |
| Ray traced shadows (`@margoengine/shadows/rt`, 2026-11-07) | Where a fragment shader cannot read four storage buffers the call answers a map filter. | No hardware path: WebGPU exposes no ray queries, as gpuweb's acceleration structure and ray query proposals are still open pull requests on 2026-11-07, so rays walk a BVH in the fragment shader and cost follows rays times lit pixels. | By method: only the tree casts, in the pose it was built; alpha masks are not in it, so a cutout casts whole triangles; shadows end at the map's reach; `Shadows` only. Against Cycles on the real plant: overlap 0.97 hard, and 0.97 under a soft sun with 64 rays, where PCSS gives 0.97. One GPU, an RTX A6000; no timing yet. | Call `bvh.refit()` then `filter.refit()` after moving what the tree holds; lower `rays` (default 16, 1 to 256) to cut cost; `fallback` picks the map filter used where storage buffers cannot be read. |
| Shadow intensity and colour (`@margoengine/shadows/style`, 2026-11-07) | Enforced: `intensity` and each of `color` from 0 to 1, one style per shadows object. | By method: art direction, so light reaches where no light could, the same light a second sun that casts nothing would add, and that is what it matches in Cycles: deep in the real plant's shadow 0.283 against 0.287 in red and 0.684 against 0.684 in blue. three.js r186's `light.shadow.intensity`, read from its pixels outside this repository, leaves 1 - intensity, the black case here; it has no colour. One GPU, an RTX A6000; no timing yet. | Speculars and everything else the light drives are scaled alike. | Leave the style off, or keep `intensity` 1 with the default black `color`, for the plain physical shadow; both options are live on `style.options`. |
| Far shadows (`@margoengine/shadows/distance`, 2026-11-07) | By method: only the height field casts, so what is not in it casts nothing past the reach, and overhangs do not exist. The march samples the one line towards the sun, bilinearly, so a spike thinner than a cell and off that line can be missed. A receiver reads the field's cells, so its edge is good to about a cell, and a point beside a one-cell wall reads a blend of the wall's cell and its own. Outside the field nothing is shaded. Y is up. The field is marched again after the sun moves `sunStep` degrees, at columns x rows x up to columns + rows reads. Against Cycles on real Rainier elevation past a `distance` of 30: overlap 0.85, mean difference 0.016. One GPU, an RTX A6000; no timing yet. | Only the height field casts, so nothing outside it casts past the reach and overhangs do not exist. | A spike thinner than a cell and off the sun line can be missed, edges are good to about a cell, and nothing outside the field is shaded. | Put buildings and other casters into `heights`; raise `columns` and `rows` for finer edges; `bias` lifts a receiver to cover the field's coarseness. |
| Light through leaves (`@margoengine/shadows/transmission`, 2026-11-07) | Transmission textures are not read: `scale` stands for them, 0.47 on that cup. The spread applies to every tint caster of its `Shadows`, coloured glass included, and needs a depth texture read, so not at WebGPU's compatibility level. `Shadows` only, as `colored` is. One GPU, an RTX A6000. | By method: one disc of `size` stands for all the scattering, a single scatter. | Against Cycles deep in the real plant's shadow Margo gives 0.0001 of the light and Cycles 0.0023, both under one 8-bit step (0.004), where light going straight on gives 0.05. A closed shell is the worst case: on the real Teacup 0.04 against 0.004, as light takes two walls there. | Set `scale` (default 1, live) to stand for a transmission texture the material has, and `size` for how far the light spreads. |
| Variance filter (`@margoengine/shadows/vsm`, 2026-11-07) | By method: one edge width everywhere, so against Cycles' soft sun on the real plant it overlaps 0.76 where PCSS overlaps 0.97. `Shadows` only. Needs `rg32float` storage textures, and takes two of them at `size`: 64 MiB at 1024 with four cascades. Cost is reasoned, not timed: a quiet speed run is still to come. Measured on one GPU, an RTX A6000. | The filter uses one edge width everywhere and stores moments in two `rg32float` storage textures. | Overlapping casters bleed light, which `bleed` trades against width. | Raise `bleed` (default 0.2, 0 to under 1) to cut light bleeding at the cost of a narrower edge, or use the `'soft'` (PCSS) filter where edges must harden towards the caster or casters overlap. |
| Light functions (`@margoengine/shadows/light-function`, 2026-11-07) | A `lightPattern` adds a sampled texture and a sampler to every receiver, which a gltf-pro material using all 16 of a stage cannot take; `effects` are fixed at construction. | The cloud layer is one flat shell and a light pattern is a sampled texture on every receiver. | By method: `cloudShadows` is one flat layer, the sky's, with no thickness: a ground point reads one spot of the shell, so cloud shadow has no soft side from cloud depth, and points above the shell get none. It follows `engine.lighting.direction`, not the sky's own sun, so the two must agree. Proved on one GPU, an RTX A6000: the pattern against Cycles on one image and one sun, the clouds against the sky's own render on 256 points at two clock times. | Use `cloudShadows` where gltf-pro materials use all 16 sampled textures (it adds one uniform buffer and no texture); `sunSize` blurs the cloud shadow's edge; the sky's `lighting` option keeps the sky's sun and `engine.lighting.direction` together. |
| Volumetric clouds (`@margoengine/sky/volumetric-clouds`, 2026-11-07) | Every pixel marches up to `steps` samples (64), and each cloudy sample takes `lightSteps` (6) more toward the sun. | By method: one layer between two heights; a full-resolution march of up to `steps` samples (64) with `lightSteps` (6) toward the sun at each cloudy one, no temporal reuse and no half-resolution pass, so cost follows pixels times steps (a 256 x 192 view took about 15 ms on a Radeon 680M, diagnostic). With multisampled depth the march stops at each pixel's first sample. The sky light is the sky's 64 x 32 reflection map, which includes the sky's own procedural layer if that is on. Proved on one GPU, a Radeon 680M, by behaviour (coverage, depth, motion), not against a reference renderer. | Fixed noise volumes (64³ and 32³) repeat every `scale` metres; static per-pixel jitter leaves a fine grain instead of bands. | Lower `steps` (and `lightSteps`) on slow GPUs, or shorten `maxDistance`; all are live on `clouds.options`. |
| Volumetric cloud shadows (`@margoengine/sky/cloud-shadows`, 2026-11-07) | By method: every lit receiver pixel marches the cloud field `samples` times (12), each with three noise reads, so cost follows lit pixels; a hard shadow of the sun's centre, with no blur from the sun's size; the light is the shadows' sun, not the sky's, so the two must agree. Points above the layer get no shadow. It keeps its own 64³ and 32³ noise volumes (about 1.2 MB of GPU memory). Proved on one GPU, a Radeon 680M, against the clouds' own view from 48 ground points. | Every lit receiver pixel marches the cloud field `samples` times, and the shadow is a hard one of the sun's centre. | Cost follows lit pixels, the shadow has no blur from the sun's size, and points above the layer get no shadow. | Lower `samples` (default 12, 2 to 64) to cut the cost per lit pixel; drive the sun from the sky's `lighting` option so the shadows' sun and the sky's agree. |
| Aerial perspective (`@margoengine/sky/aerial`, 2026-11-07) | By method: one 32 x 32 x 32 volume over the view, so the air varies smoothly across 32 screen columns and is read with the first sample of multisampled depth; beyond `maxDistance` (64 km) it holds the last slice; the planet's centre is under the camera, so a camera far from the origin sees the same air; no shadows in the air (no light shafts). Proved on one GPU, a Radeon 680M: transmittance within 0.006 of Beer-Lambert on the CPU at 1, 10 and 40 km. | The air is held in one coarse volume over the view. | On a display target the in-scattered light is blended after the display transform, an approximation. | Use it with Bloom, tonemapping or another HDR scene capture, where the blend is exact; `maxDistance` (default 64,000 m) sets how far the 32 slices reach. |
| Hillaire sky (`@margoengine/sky/hillaire`, 2026-11-07) | By method: single scattering plus Hillaire 2020's multiple scattering table, no clouds or shadows in the air; the 192 x 108 sky-view table is read bilinearly, so the horizon row blends sky and ground over one texel; the planet's centre is under the camera; the sun's disc has no limb darkening and is held at 65,504 (half-float range) against its true radiance of about 300,000 at illuminance 20. It does not light the scene (no engine sun, ambient or reflection map). Above 100 km each pixel marches 32 steps. Proved on one GPU, a Radeon 680M, by the colours physics gives (blue noon, orange sunset, ozone's twilight), not against a reference renderer. | The sky is single scattering plus a multiple scattering table read at a fixed size, with the sun disc held at the half-float range. | The horizon row blends sky and ground over one texel, and the sky does not light the scene. | Set core's sun from `sky.sun` and `sky.sunTransmittance` to light the scene, or use the root `Sky` with its `lighting` option, which drives the engine's sun and ambient. |
| Ground albedo feedback (`groundAlbedo` on the Bruneton `Sky`, 2026-11-07) | Enforced: one number or linear RGB from 0 to 1, Bruneton model only (Preetham ignores it). By method: one Lambertian albedo for the whole ground, no texture, no snow sparkle or other directional reflection; the ground's light is gathered at 32 directions per scattering density texel and a 64-entry irradiance table over the sun's elevation, as coarse as the rest of the table; the ground lit by order n - 2 feeds order n (Bruneton and Neyret 2008), and light past the fourth order, from the ground or the air, is left out as before. Proved on a Radeon 680M (LIM-375); no reference sky compared. | The Bruneton tables hold the air only; the ground is a boundary term. | A ground of mixed snow and forest takes one mean value; the brightening does not follow where the snow lies. | Use the mean albedo of the ground the view sees; `HillaireSky`'s `atmosphere.groundAlbedo` takes every order (Hillaire 2020's multiple scattering table), also as one value. |
| Planet presets (`@margoengine/sky/presets`, 2026-11-07) | Enforced: Mars's dust optical depth from 0 to 10. By method: each world is the Hillaire model's air, so Rayleigh, one Mie (dust) layer with one phase function and an ozone-like absorbing tent; Mars's dust has one asymmetry for every colour, so the blue glow around the setting sun that its wavelength-dependent forward scattering gives is not drawn; no water-ice clouds, no dust layer above the gas's scale height. The dust's green and blue single scattering albedo are Margo's reading of published curves (LIM-378). Worlds with air too thick for the 40-step tables (Venus, Titan) have no preset. | The Hillaire tables hold one Mie layer; a thick atmosphere needs more steps than they take. | A Martian sunset is a dim reddish sun in a bright halo, not blue. | Give your own `atmosphere`, per channel where needed; for thick worlds a custom sky. |
| Day presets (`@margoengine/scenery/presets`, 2026-11-07) | By method: four fixed looks (noon, golden hour, overcast, dawn) for the Preetham `Sky` with its 2D cloud sheet; the sun's colour comes from the sky's transmittance, but the overcast dimming of direct sun and sky light is a preset value (sun strength 0.5, sky 2.5 stops darker), not a cloud model; no cloud shadows; diffuse floors are Margo design values (LIM-407). | The root `Sky` has no cloud transmittance for the engine light, so a preset sets it. | Overcast is a grey, even light about a fifth of noon's; under a partly cloudy preset the sun is never hidden. | Copy a `DAY_PRESETS` entry and change its values, or add `@margoengine/sky/volumetric-clouds` and `cloud-shadows`. |
| Scene presets (`@margoengine/scenery/scenes`, 2026-11-07) | By method: four fixed looks built from the day presets; the fog is the sky's uniform exponential fog (no height layer), coloured by the clear sky's radiance in the view direction, so under the overcast cloud sheet a far object fades toward the clear-sky colour, not the cloud's; the grade is one `Tonemap` (neutral curve) with fixed exposure, not eye adaptation (LIM-409). WebGPU only, as `PostStack`. | The sky's fog reads the day model, not the cloud sheet; fixed exposure keeps the four looks repeatable. | Overcast distance fades a little bluer than the cloud above it. | Change `scene.sky.options.fog`, `scene.tonemap.options` (`autoExposure`) or `scene.bloom.options` after attaching. |
| Milky Way (`@margoengine/scenery/stars`, 2026-11-07) | By method: the band is a smooth Margo model (disc, bulge and the Great Rift's dust) with seamless noise star clouds, shaped after all-sky photometry, not a photograph of the real star clouds, the Magellanic Clouds or nebulae; no star counts below AstroSky's catalogue; no refraction of the band (under a pixel's worth above 10 degrees); WebGPU only (LIM-412). | Per-pixel maths keeps it sharp at any resolution without a large texture. | Its dark lanes and clouds do not match a photograph of the same field. | Tune `surfaceBrightness` and `clouds`; for a photographic sky, load an equirectangular image with `@margoengine/environment`. |
| Aurora (`@margoengine/scenery/aurora`, 2026-11-07) | By method: three emissions with fixed Chapman height profiles (no electron energy spectrum, no 391.4 nm or N2 first positive lower border), vertical field lines (true near the magnetic pole, tilted elsewhere), arcs along world east-west only, a Margo model of folds and rays; no light cast on the scene and no scattering in the air below; 64 steps a pixel, so curtains thinner than half a step are widened with their light kept; WebGPU only (LIM-419). | A fixed march keeps the cost to one full-screen pass. | The aurora does not light the ground or snow; a curtain seen edge on is softer than a real one. | Light the scene with a dim green `Light` yourself; raise `thickness` for softer, `intensity` and `red` for a stronger storm. |
| Light shafts (`@margoengine/sky/shafts`, 2026-11-07) | No counted threshold; the limits come from the method. | The shafts are computed in screen space, so only what is on screen takes part. | By method: screen space (Mitchell 2007), so only what is on screen occludes: a mountain or cloud off screen casts no shaft, a sun off screen still casts them from its place beyond the edge, and they fade out as the sun passes 75 degrees off the view axis; none from behind the camera or with an orthographic camera; the moon casts none.
