Not run: a rigged mesh drawn on a GPU or opened in another tool. Not measured: speed in a quiet run (0.5 to 3 s a figure without weights while other lanes ran). |
| Corrective shapes (`@margoengine/rigging/correctives`, 2026-11-06) | Enforced: 1 to 64 samples a pose space. By method: `pose` and the joint's rotation are read as rotation vectors from `rest`, so a shape reads up to half a turn from rest and the reading jumps sign past it; a clip's track on a driven target is overwritten. Not proven: no real file with correctives was found, so the proof is a real rig and clip with a stand-in morph target. Not measured: speed. |
| Weight transfer (`@margoengine/rigging/transfer`, 2026-11-06) | By method: the two meshes must be in one pose and space. Either facing passes the angle test, so a layer with its surface parallel to the wrong limb's and inside `maxDistance` takes that limb. The fill is a weighted average, so it blends and never sharpens. A target with nothing of the source around it gets the nearest weights, which can be far from an author's: 41% to 69% agreement on parts of the Twist sample with no surface around them. Not measured: speed in a quiet run. |
| Face tracking (`@margoengine/rigging/face`, 2026-11-06) | By method: morph targets and VRM expressions only, so a jaw or eye moved by a bone is not driven; the VRM presets are a fixed reading of the 52, and no value stands for `relaxed`. A clip's track on a driven target is overwritten. Not proven: no real model named with these shapes was found and no live tracker has been run; the name matching is proven on stand-in targets, the avatar path on a real VRM avatar with frames the test wrote. Not measured: speed. |
| Powered ragdolls (`@margoengine/ragdoll/powered`, 2026-11-06) | By method: joint motors keep a pose and cannot lift a character, so standing needs the `root` spring, which is an outside force. Motors add mass and inertia to small bodies (up to an eighth of the heaviest neighbour, a tenth of the heaviest limb), so a powered ragdoll is a little heavier than a limp one. A joint's torque cap and gains come from the limb's real centre of mass and inertia in the pose motors were added in, so a limb whose mass sits close to its joint gets a soft motor: with the hips pinned, Seed-san's head sags 0.080 rad and stays there, where a rod per body read 0.030, and Knight's holds at 0.000 where a rod per body fell 0.82 rad. A higher `frequency` stiffens it. The default cone clips most clips. Measured on the Rig_Medium walk at 6 Hz: mean 0.1 rad behind the clip, the worst bones (hands, feet) 0.5 rad at peak. Not proven: Rapier 0.21 is the only version run; no timing yet. |
| Partial ragdolls (`Ragdoll.activate(limb)`, 2026-11-06) | By method: a limb is a bone and everything under it, so a hand without its fingers takes a list of parts of `ragdoll.bones` instead. A limb hangs from bodies that reach each frame's pose at that frame's physics step, so it is drawn up to one step behind a fast parent. A bone of the limb under a limp parent that is handed back alone follows its parent's drawn pose a frame late. |
| Getting up (`@margoengine/ragdoll/getup`, 2026-11-06) | By method: two clips, chosen at the root bone only, so a body on its side takes whichever is nearer. `root` is a top-level node. The character turns about the up axis only and the blend covers the rest; a limb far from the clip's pose crosses the body during the blend, with no collision. Not proven on an authored get-up clip: none is in the repository, so the proof plays the rig's Death_A and Death_B backward (stand-in clips, real rig, real physics). |
| Body shapes from the skin (`@margoengine/ragdoll/hulls`, 2026-11-06) | By method: one skin is read, the first that holds the root bone, since skins have separate bind spaces. A bone the skin has no bind pose for keeps its capsule. Vertices shared evenly by bones count for none at the default `minWeight`, so a hull can stop short of a joint. A hull is convex: a bone that carries a concave part overshoots into the hollow. The box lies along the bone, the smallest over 18 turns 5 degrees apart. Measured on Knight.glb, ray silhouette overlap with the mesh over a front and a side view: capsules 0.13, boxes 0.65, hulls 0.93; on Fox.glb, capsules 0.43, hulls 0.78. Thicker shapes reach the floor sooner: with limits the body cannot meet lying down, such as the default quarter-turn cone on a T-posed arm, a limit and a contact fight and the limb keeps moving (hand at about 0.8 m/s after 16 s on Knight.glb, limits held to under 1 rad over). Skipping the limit correction on touching limbs settled it, but let a spine fold 2.3 rad past its limit, so it is not done; give a fitted body limits it can reach (`swing` 2.5 settles under 0.05 m/s). |
| Saved profiles (`@margoengine/ragdoll/profile`, 2026-11-06) | By method: bones bind by node name, so a renamed rig needs its profile edited. Limits are measured from the pose the ragdoll is built in, so a profile fits only that pose; the humanoid preset reads a T or A pose and its hinges and ranges are wrong for any other. One cone per joint is narrower than a real shoulder or hip in some directions and wider in others. Mass shares are one published adult male table, de Leva 1996. A hinge is a knee or an elbow only: spines, shoulders and ankles are cones. Proved on Knight.glb, with Mixamo names only as stand-in names on the same rig. |
| Balance (`@margoengine/ragdoll/balance`, 2026-11-06) | By method: it stands on joint torques alone by default, every one a pair inside the body with the ankle held to what the sole answers; `carry` is an outside force, off unless set. It adds inertia to feet and shins (0.012 of the whole mass times the leg's length squared) brings the character's island to 8 solver passes at the least, and gives a hull foot a flat sole of four corners while it balances. Its torques are forces through the whole step, so a world set above 8 only helps: over 24 roundings at 8, 10 and 16 passes no figure steps late or falls, and no hips drift 0.0020 m. A stance knee takes its torque from the balance with a spring beside it that has no damper and grows with how far the knee is off its bend, full at 0.15 rad, so `motors.setStrength` does not reach it. It stands and steps at the heading it was added at: no turning or walking. Flat ground only, at the height of the feet; no stairs or slopes. `motors.hit` weakens a limb's motor, not the balance torques, which follow the global `strength` only. Measured over 24 hull roundings each at 8 passes: all seven figures settle with no step after the first second, at 0.0006 m/s at most. Worst drift of the hips over five seconds: Knight.glb 0.0009 m, Barbarian 0.0004, Ranger 0.0006, Rogue 0.0010, Mage 0.0003, Seed-san 0.0019, RiggedFigure 0.0005; no foot slides more than 0.0012 m. On 96 other roundings at 8 passes one Seed-san foot slid 0.0020 m and 8 of the 672 figure runs shook a bone over 0.005 m/s; at 10, 16 and 20 passes none did (known-bugs.md, "A standing figure can shake"). Pushes of 1.5 m/s in four directions over 32 roundings on Knight.glb: 125 of 128 pass the push test's reading and none fall; the old assist passed 122 and fell in none, holding about half the weight from outside. The other three are still stepping six seconds after the push and stand still by fourteen. Those 128 pushes at 8 / 10 / 16 passes, failing the reading / falling within four seconds: Knight 3/0, 1/0, 7/0; Rogue 1/0, 4/0, 2/0; Ranger 7/0, 8/0, 8/0; Mage 21/0, 25/0, 13/0; Barbarian 29/9, 36/14, 43/30; RiggedFigure 96/95, 92/88, 96/96; Seed-san 113/108, 121/120, 128/128. Falls in 128 pushes at 8 passes at 0.5 / 1 / 1.5 m/s: Knight, Ranger, Rogue and Mage 0 / 0 / 0, Barbarian 0 / 8 / 9, RiggedFigure 60 / 63 / 95, Seed-san 69 / 93 / 108, so only the first four stay up at 1.5 m/s and RiggedFigure and Seed-san do not reliably take 0.5 (known-bugs.md, "A push throws"). Not measured: speed, including the cost of the added passes. |
| Uneven bone scales in ragdolls (`@margoengine/ragdoll`, 2026-11-06) | Enforced: zero and mirrored bones are refused. By method: any positive scale is taken. Bodies are rigid and sized in world units when the ragdoll is built, so a scale animated afterwards does not resize them. A bone's own uneven scale costs nothing: a fall with a stretched spine matches the same rig built unstretched to under 1 mm. A bone turned under an uneven scale above it is skewed, so the bones drawn below it stretch as it turns, as in IK, while the bodies keep their joint distance; the pose holds exactly where the bone went limp and drifts from its body as it turns away. A hull fitted to a skewed bone takes the scales of the bone's axes and not the skew. Proved on Seed-san's own scales (`hair_E`, 0.9995, 1, 0.9986); scales as large as 0.1, 3, 40 only on a stand-in rig. |
| Swept collision (`@margoengine/kinematics-constraints/swept`, 2026-11-06) | By method: colliders hold still during a check, so a moving obstacle needs its own stepping. The path checked is the shortest arc per bone for a chain, and the shortest arc about the hull's origin for a hull; if your animation plays another path, this one is certified, not yours. A contact is any gap at or below `minClearance` plus `tolerance` (1e-4), and a motion that starts there stops at time 0, so keep `minClearance` below the gap the pose was solved with. A gap of `d` and a speed bound of `s` give a step of `d / s`, so a motion that grazes a surface for a long way takes many steps: past the budget of 4,096 poses it ends `exhausted`, which is not clear; raise `maxSteps` or `tolerance`. |
| Concave obstacles (`@margoengine/kinematics-constraints/sdf`, 2026-11-06) | By method: `SDFVolume` is only as fine as its grid. A wall thinner than a cell may not be in the field at all, and the default margin of one cell's diagonal reads every obstacle that much fatter; a grid must hold the whole obstacle, because past its edge a reading is the distance to the grid. Both volumes take a rotation, one uniform scale and a move, no shear. A crossing segment reads minus its radius and an overlapping hull reads zero or less on a mesh, which ranks poses and is not a depth. Mesh queries prune with a box round the query, so a long diagonal link visits more of the tree than it needs. `/sdf` is the one entry that loads `@margoengine/bvh/inside`, for the sign of a solid mesh. |
| `p-morph` build plus first frame (accepted only for now, open in [known-bugs.md](known-bugs.md#build-plus-first-frame-of-100-morphed-copies-is-still-slower-than-threes-webgl-backend)) | 154 ms, or 125 to 143 with `PBRModel.compile()` (first frame 17), against three's WebGPU 217 and WebGL 54, busy-machine diagnostic. |
| `PBRModel.compile()` | Compiles for the canvas pass under the lights and effects set when it is called. A later change to either, a batched textureless mesh, and any pass other than beauty compile on the draw as before. |

## Platform

| Limit | Starts at | Why | What you see | Escape hatch |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Docs resources and starter prompts (`@margoengine/mcp/resources`, 2026-11-08) | Enforced: lists are paged, 100 items a page by default (`pageSize` of 1 or more); a cursor the server did not hand out is `-32602`; a prompt's required argument must be a non-blank string; only the addresses the snapshot lists are served, anything else is `-32002`. By method: the resources are the README, package READMEs and API reports and docs pages copied at package build (257 resources, about 8.3 MB of markdown, in the build of 2026-11-08), so they match the installed version and do not change while the server runs: no subscriptions or change notifications. The prompts are fixed text with the arguments put in; they steer the model and check nothing themselves. | A snapshot answers for the version you use; four prompts cover the common starts without a menu to learn. | `resources/list` pages with `nextCursor`; `resources/read` gives `text/markdown`; `prompts/get` gives one user message. | `createMcpServer({ resources, prompts })` with your own `ResourceProvider` and `PromptDefinition`s. |
| Render a snippet (`render_snippet` in `@margoengine/mcp/render`, 2026-11-08) | Enforced: 16 to 2048 pixels a side (default 512), a time limit of 1 to 300 s (default 60 s) after which the process is killed, snippets up to 200,000 characters, the console cut to its last 4,000 characters; offered only with `--render`. By method: a child Node process per call, so each render pays process start and GPU device creation (about 1 s on the RTX A6000); TypeScript has its types stripped, not checked (Node 22.13 or newer); the snippet gets a headless stage, so code that needs a page, input or a frame loop does not run. | Running in its own process keeps a hung or crashing snippet from taking the server down. | A PNG and a line with its size, time and drawn pixels; `failed` with the snippet's error, or `unsupported` when the project lacks a package. | `renderSnippet` with your own `timeoutMs`, `gpuFlags` for a software adapter; `@margoengine/headless` directly for many frames in one process. |
| Error lookup (`explain_error` in `@margoengine/mcp/error-codes`, 2026-11-08) | Enforced: `limit` from 1 to 10 entries (default 3). By method: an entry is found by its link, id or `MargoError` code, a listed message word for word, or else the listed message sharing the most words (at least half of the asked words); a message the page does not list and that shares few words gets no entry. The entries are copied from the error help page at package build, so an installed package answers for its own version. | The page is the one source of every message's cause and fix. | The entry's title, link, cause and fix; "No error help entry matches" otherwise. | Pass the `errors#<id>` link or the code, which always match. |
| Porting guide (`port_to_margo` in `@margoengine/mcp/porting`, 2026-11-08) | Enforced: `limit` from 1 to 25 rows (default 3 in the tool, 5 in `lookupPorting`); `from` only one of the libraries the docs hold a matrix for (today one, three.js r186: 195 rows in 19 areas, 27 guide sections). By method: rows match by words and the parts of camelCase names, with plural and verb endings dropped, not by meaning; a name the matrix does not list gets no row; a guide section is attached only when it shares a title word or four text words with the row. The guides are copied from `docs/` at package build, so an installed package answers for its own version. | The matrix and guide are the source of truth, and a docs page is easier to keep current than a mapping in code. | A row with its status, Margo calls, notes and, where the guide has a section, both libraries' code; otherwise "No ... row matches" and a pointer to `search_docs`. | `search_docs` for anything the matrix does not name; `parsePortingGuide` and `createMargoServer({ porting })` for your own matrix. |
| Boxes and capsules in the rewind (`@margoengine/netcode/hitboxes`, 2026-11-07) | Enforced: `history` from 2 to 4,096 ticks; sizes of 0 or more; a rotation of non-zero length. | By method: 3D only, so a 2D game passes z as 0; every shape is tested in turn, with no broad phase, so the cost grows with the number of shapes; a shape moves in a straight line between ticks, as in `LagCompensation`; one id is one shape, so a body of several parts uses several ids. Rays only: no sweep of a moving shot or an area | A shot at a turning box or capsule counts where the shooter saw it | `LagCompensation` for circles and spheres, in the root entry |
| Engine scene binding (`@margoengine/save/engine`, 2026-11-07) | Enforced: a save is checked whole before anything changes; a plugin in the save and not in the scene needs a `type`; the binding's component names are refused for yours. By method: geometry is written into the save as base64, about 32 bytes a vertex and 5.3 an index before gzip, with no table of named geometry, so a big mesh belongs in a model. A model comes back from its URL, which must still serve the same file: node indices are matched, not names. A node within two float32 steps of its file pose counts as not moved; a node given by a `matrix` in its file is always saved. Rotations go through `setQuaternion`, which normalises again, so a value can move by two float32 steps per level of the tree. A model not loaded from a URL needs `modelURL`. A restore holds the old and new models at once until the swap. Plugins: the default reads only `saveState`, JSON `options` and getter and setter pairs, because public fields cannot be told from private ones at run time, so a field such as `Animation.time` needs `properties`; a plugin with nothing readable is reported to `onSkip` and left out; nothing in the plugin set has `saveState` yet; a plugin is matched by `name`, so one whose name has a running number needs the same name on load or its own loading; `options` apply only when a `type` attaches the plugin. A `type` attaches to the engine's current scene. A mesh a plugin owns, other than a model's, is saved like any mesh unless `filter` leaves it out. Textures and custom shaders of a `@margoengine/materials` mesh are not saved. A captured file's geometry and flags mean nothing to `addScene`, which reads its models and transforms. Stand-in: hierarchies, plugins and failures run on a real engine with a stand-in renderer and stand-in models. Not measured: no timed number. | LIM-411. Geometry is written into the save as base64, and public fields cannot be told from private ones at run time. | A plugin with nothing readable is reported to `onSkip` and left out, and a node within two float32 steps of its file pose counts as not moved. | A big mesh belongs in a model; a field such as `Animation.time` needs `properties`; a model not loaded from a URL needs `modelURL`. |
| Save slots (`@margoengine/save/slots`, 2026-11-07) | Enforced: `auto-` ids belong to the ring; a `namespace` holds no `:`. `captureThumbnail` needs `OffscreenCanvas`, and a WebGPU canvas must be taken in the task of its frame. (LIM-413) | By method: a slot is one save file, an optional thumbnail file and a record, so a save with a thumbnail is three writes, and the old slot stands until the third. | That holds where one write of the storage is atomic (`@margoengine/save/slots` adds no checksum, so a torn record shows as `damaged`, and a torn save fails on load). Two tabs or processes on one storage are not coordinated: the write order holds inside one `SaveSlots`. A `savedAt` comes from the clock, and the ring takes the oldest by it, so a clock set back can pick a newer autosave. Stand-in: failed writes are a stand-in storage. Not measured: no timed number; one GPU, one browser. | Wrap the storage in `safeStorage` from `@margoengine/save/safe` so a torn write falls back to the last good copy, and pass `now` to `SaveSlots` for a clock that never goes back. |
| Safe writes (`@margoengine/save/safe`, 2026-11-07) | Enforced: a write goes only over the copy that is not the newest good one; both copies bad rejects, never gives bad bytes. By method: two copies, so a key takes twice the room; a write is two reads, a write and a read back, each of the whole save. A failed read of the newest copy counts as damage, so the write after it can go over that copy. CRC-32 finds accidents and is no signature: the 802.3 polynomial has Hamming distance 4 up to 91,607 bits (about 11 KB), and 3 at least to 131,072 bits (Koopman, DSN 2002), so on a bigger save every 1 or 2 bit error and every burst of 32 bits is found, and other damage slips past about once in 2^32. Two processes on one key are not coordinated. A key ending in `~a` or `~b` of a plain save reads as a copy. Files (`fileStorage`): a key's file name, escaped, must fit the file system, usually 255 bytes; a kill leaves a `.tmp` file behind, which no call clears; on Windows a save another program has open cannot be replaced, so the write rejects with the old save in place. Stand-in: prefixes, torn and reordered writes are built on a stand-in storage from the paper's crash model. Real: a process killed during writes of real files, 12 times each, on Windows NTFS, macOS APFS and Linux ext4, which also ran the real folder fsync (run by hand on the Mac and on Ubuntu, not in `npm test`). Not proved: power loss, which nothing here has tested, so that the fsync really orders the disk is taken from the system's promise. Not measured: no timed number. | Two copies are kept, and a write goes only over the copy that is not the newest good one. | On Windows a save another program has open cannot be replaced, so the write rejects with the old save in place. | Close the other program that holds the save open and save again. The old save stands until then. |
| Cloud saves (`@margoengine/save/remote`, 2026-11-07) | Enforced: a write needs a base, the tag of a read, or `If-None-Match: *`; a weak tag or none cannot be a base and is refused with the reason; a conflict takes at most 5 tries; a `Retry-After` over a minute fails the call. `remoteHandler` takes 64 MiB by default. | A write is conditional on a tag, and tags live in memory. | By method: tags live in memory, so a new page or device reads before it writes, or the write counts as a conflict. A delete of a key never read is unconditional. A function policy merges the whole save, in memory, and may run again if the server moves on. A write is a whole save: no partial upload, no resume, no compression of its own. Each attempt's body is held in memory. `remoteHandler` reads a save to tag it on each request, and its turns hold in one process: two processes on one storage can split a precondition from its write. It has no auth of its own; `authorize` is yours. Keys: `keys()` needs a JSON array, which WebDAV servers do not serve. Apache with mod_dav sends no tag with a PUT and a weak one for about a second after a change, so a second write in that second is refused. Real: Chromium through real CORS against `node:http`, and Apache 2.4.67 with mod_dav on macOS, by hand. Stand-in: lost answers, 429 and 503 are a scripted fetch. Not measured: no timed number. | Read the key first on a new device, or set the `conflict` option (`'local'`, `'remote'` or a function that merges) so a first write does not fail as a conflict. |
| Rollback relay (`@margoengine/netcode/relay`, 2026-11-07) | Rollback sessions past a full mesh of about 8, and spectators who cost the players nothing Enforced: `maxSpectators` (default 256), `history` (default 216,000 frames). | By method: every message takes two hops, so latency is the sum of two links; the relay carries all traffic and is one place that can fail, with no failover. It trusts the player id a connection names unless `authorize` checks it. A player who leaves ends the frames spectators get, since a frame needs every player's input. 16 peers is what is proved, on a stand-in network; the relay's load at that size is not measured | Every message takes two hops, so latency is the sum of two links. | A direct full mesh for up to about 8 players, for one hop less |
| Extrapolation (`@margoengine/netcode/extrapolate`, 2026-11-07) | Enforced: `maxTicks` and `blend` of 0 or more. By method: a straight line from the last two snapshots, with no acceleration or game knowledge, so it overshoots turns and stops, and a teleport in the newest snapshot is flung on for `maxTicks`. A blend can show a jump as a fast glide through walls: it is drawing only, with no collision test. Lag compensation rewinds to the interpolated view, so a shot at a guessed position is judged against where the target really was | A straight line from the last two snapshots has no acceleration or game knowledge. | Remote entities keep moving through a short gap in snapshots | `client.interpolated()` holds still instead, with no wrong guesses |
| Signalling (`@margoengine/netcode/signal`, 2026-11-07) | Enforced: `maxRooms`, `maxPeers`, `maxMessageBytes`, `messagesPerSecond` with `burst`, `joinTimeout`. By method: rooms live in one process's memory, with no history, so a peer that joins late hears only what is sent after. `PeerMesh` is a full mesh, sensible up to about 8 peers; it sets up each link once and does no ICE restart or renegotiation. Only Chromium on loopback is proved. | Rooms are held in the memory of one signalling process, and no STUN or TURN server is bundled, so `iceServers` are yours to provide. | Without `iceServers` peers link on the same network only; no STUN or TURN server comes with it. | Carry the messages yourself and use `createDataChannels` |
| Reconnection (`@margoengine/netcode/rooms`, 2026-11-07) | By method: a seat is held in the memory of the one server process, so a server restart loses it. The token is a bearer secret: whoever holds it takes the seat. The client gets a new `JoinedRoom`, so listeners are added again, and it reconnects when your code calls `reconnect`, not by itself. A seat is held only for the client that had it; reserving a seat for a player who has not joined yet is not built. The join reply gained a field, so a rooms client and server must be the same version | A seat is held in the memory of the one server process. | A dropped player rejoins the same room and seat within `reconnectWindow` | Join again for a new seat |
| WebTransport (`@margoengine/netcode/webtransport`, 2026-11-07) | Enforced: a reliable message over `maxMessageBytes` (default 16 MiB) closes the connection; `unsupported` where the class is missing. | Snapshots as real datagrams without ICE or signalling, where an HTTP/3 host exists | By method: this is the client and a server-side wrapper only. Node has no HTTP/3 server, so the server host is supplied by you and must hand over a session of the W3C shape; `serve()` does not run on it. Only Chromium 156 is proved, on loopback; no real network, no other browser. The test server is aioquic, which needed a private settings method to announce what Chromium asks for, so another host's settings are its own affair. One reliable stream per connection, so reliable messages keep one order. Chromium reported a `maxDatagramSize` of 1,211 B on loopback, so a snapshot over that goes on the reliable stream and loses the benefit: keep them small with `InterestManager` budgets. Datagrams may be lost, reordered or repeated, which the snapshot protocol already takes | `openWebSocket` where there is no WebTransport or no HTTP/3 host; WebRTC for peer to peer |
| Correction smoothing (`@margoengine/netcode/smoothing`, 2026-11-07) | Enforced: `halfLife`, `snap` and `settle` above 0, `fast.to` above `fast.from`. | By method: it smooths `client.predicted` only, not `interpolated()`, which has no corrections to smooth; numbers only, so a value the schema steps jumps at once; an angle's offset is added without wrapping, so a drawn angle can pass pi by the offset; a struct's distance adds its `'linear'` fields, so fields of different units in one struct count as one distance. What is drawn is behind the truth while blending, so a hit test on the screen's position can differ from the simulation by `smooth.error`. One clock step per `state()` call: call it once a frame | The player's box eases to the corrected place over a few frames | `snap` for teleports, `reset()` at a respawn, a shorter `halfLife` for a tighter feel |
| Interest and budgets (`@margoengine/netcode/interest`, 2026-11-07) | Enforced: `radius`, `cell` and `budget` above 0, `leaveRadius` at least `radius`, positions of 2 or 3 finite numbers, top-level maps of structs. By method: the budget is per snapshot message, not a rate, and no pacing happens between snapshots; the own entity and every field not managed always go, so a budget smaller than those is passed (`stats(player).over`). A snapshot is a delta against what the client confirmed, so a value is repeated until confirmed: under a tight budget a moving entity updates about once per round trip plus one snapshot, and the budget buys that much less than its bytes suggest. If repeats alone pass a budget that was lowered, or an old baseline is lost, an entity can show the older confirmed value for a few snapshots, and a snapshot in full holds only what fits. The cost of each entity is an encode per client per snapshot; no timed run yet. A game step that reads entities out of scope mispredicts on the client and is corrected | The budget is per snapshot message, and a snapshot is a delta against what the client confirmed, so a value is repeated until confirmed. | A client's entities arrive in turn; far or low-priority ones update less often | Raise `budget`, pass a function of the player, or lower `snapshotEvery`; write your own `view` for a different rule |
| One timeline (`@margoengine/net/timeline`, 2026-11-07) | Enforced: 2 to 65,536 entries, times strictly rising; `add` refuses an older or repeated time. By method: a full ring drops its oldest entry, and `GameClient` keeps `history` snapshots, up to 4,096. An `AdaptiveDelay` passed to `GameClient` is reset when the client is made, so one instance serves one client; it counts snapshot ticks as server time, so it needs no ping. Stand-in: the client's view was proved on `LoopbackNetwork`. Not measured: no timed number. | One ring for four users removes the duplicates; each entry costs a search of O(log n). | The default view of `GameClient` is unchanged; a measured delay is opt-in. | Pass `new AdaptiveDelay()` as `interpolationDelay`; LIM-379 |
| Any field interpolation (`@margoengine/net/fields`, 2026-11-07) | Enforced: 1 to 64 numbers a field, colour 3 or 4, 2 to 65,536 snapshots, `maxPrediction` 0 to 10 s. By method: a looping `time` takes the shorter way round, so a clip must advance by under half its period between snapshots to read as forward; a clip restarted without a change of its `clip` field reads as a scrub back; across a change of clip the time holds until the new snapshot's moment; `linear`, `angle`, `color` and `step` hold past the newest snapshot, and only `hermite` and `time` run on. Not measured: no timed number. | A snapshot holds no more than two samples of a clock, so the blend cannot tell a fast clip from a backward one. | A clip can seem to hold for one snapshot interval at a change. | Send a new `clip` number on every restart; LIM-377 |
| Adaptive render delay (`@margoengine/net/delay`, 2026-11-07) | Enforced: `quantile` 0.5 to 1, `window` 16 to 65,536 arrivals, delay from `min` to `max` (default 0 to 1 s). By method: the delay covers a share of recent arrivals, so at the default 99% about one snapshot in a hundred lands late and the view holds or extrapolates; the first 8 arrivals keep `initial` as a floor; the smallest transit in the window stands for the link's fixed delay, so a clock drifting against the sender lifts the delay by the drift across the window; a change of delay shows as motion up to 25% slow or 5% fast, as a game has no silence to hide it in; a spike longer than a quarter of the window counts as a new route, and the link is measured afresh. Stand-in: proved on seeded simulated links, not a captured network trace. Not measured: no timed number. | Those are the published methods, with Moon's percentile over a far shorter window than its 10,000 packets, as a game link can change within seconds. | A late snapshot shows as a hold or a short extrapolation. A view that trails by more than `max` jumps forward. | `quantile`, `window`, `margin`, `min`, `max` and the two rates; LIM-373 |
| WebGPU compatibility level (`@margoengine/webgl2-compat/adapter`, 2026-11-05, packages 2026-11-06) | Checked on a forced device (Chromium, RTX A6000, D3D12), not yet on a real OpenGL ES or Direct3D 11 class GPU. Runs: core (same pixels as the full level), pick, device restore, materials `createMesh` and `createInstancedMesh`, `Shadows` (same pixels as the full level, soft shadow on a floor), `VirtualShadows` with `'soft'`, `CulledInstances`, `PostStack` with `Bloom` and `Tonemap`, points with eye-dome lighting (same pixels as the full level). `SurfelPlugin` at 4x refuses with `unsupported`. | The level forbids `textureLoad` of depth texture types, `sample_index` and a plain sampler on a depth texture, two views of one texture in a dispatch, and multisampled `rgba16float` and `r32float`. The adapter rewrites depth textures that are only loaded to float types; `Shadows` and `VirtualShadows` bind their map twice, `SceneTarget` copies depth with a masked draw per sample, eye-dome lighting shades the same way and packs its depth into `rgba8unorm` in a second pass of the same draws, `Bloom` keeps each level in its own texture, and `PostStack` captures 4x in `rg11b10ufloat`. | Accepted difference: that capture has less precision and no alpha, measured at most 2 of 255 from the full level on the Bloom and Tonemap frame, 4,066 of 16,384 bytes differing. An adapter without `rg11b10ufloat-renderable` gets a validation error at a 4x capture. Fog, volumetrics, points, water spray and soft `VirtualShadows` are in [known-bugs.md](known-bugs.md). A real older GPU also has its own lower limits, which a forced run cannot show. | Install `@margoengine/webgl2-compat` instead on such a device: all of these run there. |
| Rendering in a worker (`@margoengine/worker`, 2026-11-05) | Proved on Chromium with a real GPU: core, a control package, the stage class and the WebGL2 fallback run on a transferred canvas with no core change, and neither thread stops the other ([browser-check.mjs](../packages/worker/tests/browser-check.mjs)). Firefox and Safari have not been run. | A worker has no DOM: it cannot stop a browser default in time, and pointer lock stays on the page. | The page stops `wheel` and `contextmenu` defaults once the worker listens for them, and no other; input arrives one message later; `mount` needs a page. | `new Stage(engine)` in the worker; a page path behind `workerCanvasSupported()`. |
| Picking on the WebGL2 preview (`@margoengine/webgl/pick`, 2026-11-06) | Proved in Chromium on real WebGL2: CSS pixels, depth, single and double-sided faces, hidden meshes, views, layers, a canvas left unchanged, `Picking` and `HoverHighlight` from `selection`, which answer with the mesh only and take one pick a point in `pickMany`, and React pointer handlers ([browser-check.mjs](../packages/webgl/test/browser-check.mjs)). Firefox and Safari have not been run. Mesh ids up to 16,777,216, the largest whole number a 32-bit float holds. | Core's pick refuses every renderer but WebGPU's before any plugin hook, so the plugin covers `pick` and `render` on that one engine; core stays frozen. | Each pick blocks on one `readPixels` and refreshes the scene as a frame does. A plugin's `onPick` is not called, and `Picking` from `@margoengine/selection` still needs WebGPU. | `engine.pick` on WebGPU or the compatibility layer. |
| Context restore on the WebGL2 preview (`@margoengine/webgl/restore`, 2026-11-06) | Proved in Chromium with `WEBGL_lose_context`: identical pixels after a loss, picks, geometry added during the loss, `onDeviceRestored` in each scene, and a reboot on a live context ([browser-check.mjs](../packages/webgl/test/browser-check.mjs)). Firefox and Safari have not been run. `rebootDevice` waits 30,000 ms by default (`timeout`) for a lost context. | The browser decides whether a lost context returns, and the plugin must be attached before the loss, or the browser never gives it back. | Frames draw nothing during a loss. A timed-out wait reports `timeout`, and a later restore still recovers. An app that draws on demand draws again itself, from `onDeviceRestored`. | A new engine on a new canvas. |
| XR on the WebGL2 compatibility layer (`@margoengine/webgl2-compat/xr`, 2026-11-06) | Run on real devices with stand-ins for `navigator.xr`, `XRGPUBinding` and `XRWebGLLayer`: no pixel differs from native WebGPU in either eye (Chromium, RTX A6000, [xr-browser.mjs](../packages/webgl2-compat/test/xr-browser.mjs)). No headset or phone has run it, and Firefox and Safari have not been run. | WebGL2 sessions show an `XRWebGLLayer`, whose framebuffer takes draws only, and its depth buffer cannot be filled from another texture. | One extra full-eye copy a frame, and no depth for the compositor to reproject by. The first frame that makes a compute pipeline shows nothing while the compute runtime loads. | `installXR({ framebufferScale })` to trade sharpness for speed; WebGPU where the browser has it. |
| WebGPU first | Enforced: `Engine.create` uses WebGPU unless the app passes a fallback ([engine.ts](../packages/core/src/engine.ts), [webgl.test.js](../packages/webgl/test/webgl.test.js), [webgl-preview.test.js](../tests/webgl-preview.test.js)). | One backend keeps core + stage at 23,626 B gzip; culling, particles, sections and most effects are compute or WebGPU-only work. | `Error: WebGPU unavailable` (no fallback) or `Error: adapter unavailable` (`backend: 'webgpu'`); on the WebGL2 path, `engine.pick` and `engine.rebootDevice` throw `WebGPU required` (core is frozen, so a plain `Error`), and packages that need WebGPU throw `MargoError` code `unsupported` naming themselves when attached (`PostProcessing requires WebGPU; ...`, `VFX ...`, `Sky ...`, `Shadows ...`). On margoengine.com, a browser without WebGPU (no `navigator.gpu`, or no adapter) downloads the WebGL2 compatibility layer and runs every live demo on it (see the next row); only a browser without WebGL2 at the layer's full tier as well gets "This demo needs a browser with WebGPU: Chrome, Edge or Safari." in the canvas area with a link to [webgpu.html](../site/webgpu.html) on what to update, and downloads no demo code; the landing page draws no scene. No demo runs on the small WebGL2 preview: each needs effects, particles, skinning or compute, and all of them run on the layer. | `@margoengine/webgl` (3,154 B gzip, stays for v1.0 as the small preview, `Engine.create(canvas, {backend: 'auto', fallback: webglRenderer})`): a basic preview of core meshes, shaded per pixel with one directional light plus ambient, no textures, effects or picking. Checked in a real browser with WebGPU removed (`npm run check:no-webgpu`, part of `check:merge`: every site demo installs the layer without WebGPU, shows the message without WebGL2 too, downloads none of the layer with WebGPU, throws no uncaught error, and the preview draws two coloured boxes, read back as pixels) and in [gates-browser-tests.json](../examples/package-benchmarks/results/gates-browser-tests.json). The WebGL2 compatibility layer (`@margoengine/webgl2-compat`) is what the site runs without WebGPU, and the fuller fallback: it runs the WebGPU code of core and the packages as an optional download that WebGPU users never load ([webgl2-compat-roadmap.md](webgl2-compat-roadmap.md)). In React, `loadFallback` fetches the basic fallback only without WebGPU and `fallbackContent` replaces the plain message. |
| Automatic escalation from the preview (`@margoengine/webgl/auto`, 2026-11-06) | Proved in Chromium with WebGPU removed and with it present ([browser-check.mjs](../packages/webgl/test/browser-check.mjs)). Firefox and Safari have not been run. Only a refusal (code `unsupported`) during `setup` escalates, and only with `loadCompat`. | The layer cannot show frames on a canvas that holds the preview's context, and a canvas keeps its first context, so the element is replaced with a copy; listeners on the old element do not carry over. The app passes the import because no package may name the layer ([isolation.test.js](../packages/webgl2-compat/test/isolation.test.js)). | `setup` runs twice when escalating. A package added after `setup` refuses as usual. The React `Canvas` does not escalate: its `loadCompat` prop installs the layer before the engine, so it sets up once and keeps its canvas, at the cost of the layer's download where the preview would have done. | Install the layer yourself before `Engine.create`, as its README shows. |
| WebGPU pages alive at once | Measured on an Intel Arc 140V laptop (Edge and Chrome 156, headless, 2026-10-30): with 3 WebGPU pages alive in one browser process a new page's `requestDevice` took 10.6 s, and the 4th page never got a device or finished its pipeline compile (4 of 7 runs); 2 pages at once, one page at a time, or 4 separate browsers always started. The NVIDIA RTX A6000 desktop starts 4 at once. Enforced since then: each `Engine.create` startup step (`requestAdapter`, `requestDevice`, each pipeline compile) has a limit ([renderer.ts](../packages/core/src/renderer.ts), [core-startup-watchdog.test.js](../tests/core-startup-watchdog.test.js)). | The stall is in the driver or the browser's GPU process, not the page: the pages already drawing keep drawing every frame while the new one waits. | One `console.warn` (`Margo: still waiting for requestDevice after 8 s; see https://margoengine.com/errors#timeout`) after `startupWarning` ms (default 8,000), then a rejection with `MargoError` code `timeout` naming the step after `startupTimeout` ms (default 30,000); `backend: 'auto'` with a `fallback` draws on WebGL2 instead. Before, `Engine.create` never settled and the canvas stayed blank at 300 x 150. | Start several engines one at a time (each after the previous has drawn) and dispose those that leave the screen; the demo gallery must not start many engines at once. `signal` cancels creation; raise `startupTimeout` for a slow first compile. |
| Compute on the WebGL2 compatibility layer | Measured (Edge 156, ANGLE Direct3D 11, NVIDIA RTX A6000, 2026-10-28, [compute.html](../packages/webgl2-compat/test/compute.html) cases and a timing page). Compute entry points that store only their own texel run as fragment draws and those with a bounded number of stores as point draws, on the GPU. The rest (atomics, workgroup memory, barriers, stores in loops of unknown length) run on the CPU in JavaScript: a clustered-binning kernel over 3,456 clusters takes 1.5 ms with 16 lights, 2.8 ms with 64 and 9.4 ms with 256 (37 to 94 million loop iterations a second); a 64-wide workgroup reduction with 7 barriers runs 4.4 million invocations a second (65,536 in 14.9 ms). | WebGL2 has no compute shaders, storage buffers or atomics. | The same results as WebGPU (the layer's compute check compares both on one GPU), slower: GPU-written inputs of a CPU kernel are read back synchronously (counted in the device's `stalls`). Multisampled and external textures cannot be read by CPU-tier kernels (MargoError `unsupported`); cube textures are sampled there as on WebGPU, seamless; CPU kernels compile with `new Function` where the page allows it (CSP `unsafe-eval`) and otherwise run on the interpreter of the next row. A NaN that a CPU-tier kernel stores, bitcasts or packs is written as the one quiet NaN 0x7fc00000 (f16 0x7e00), so its sign and payload are not kept, which WGSL 15.7.2 allows (any value) and which keeps the bytes the same on every engine and on the interpreter. A CPU-tier dispatch stops at its work budget, 100,000,000 invocations plus loop iterations by default (`cpuWorkBudget`), and is reported as a `GPUInternalError` coded `failed` with nothing stored. | Keep per-frame CPU-tier work under the numbers measured here; [webgl2-compat-roadmap.md](webgl2-compat-roadmap.md) lists the tiers. A transform-feedback tier for own-index stores is parked (speed only: those stores already run on the scatter tier with the same pixels, about 0.09 ms more per stored word, measured in the roadmap). |
| CPU compute tier without `unsafe-eval` (`@margoengine/webgl2-compat`, 2026-11-06) | Checked: all 372 buffer-only corpus kernels leave the same memory on the interpreter as compiled ([interpret.test.js](../packages/webgl2-compat/test/interpret.test.js)), and every [compute.html](../packages/webgl2-compat/test/compute.html) case, textures included, matches native WebGPU under a real policy (Chromium, RTX A6000, [compute-browser.mjs](../packages/webgl2-compat/test/compute-browser.mjs)). Firefox and Safari have not been run. Speed, diagnostic because other jobs ran: 41.2 times slower on a 256-light binning loop, 13.5 times on a reduction with barriers. | A page that forbids `new Function` leaves only walking the program: the interpreter parses the JavaScript the CPU tier writes and runs it as closures. | Per-frame CPU-tier work costs 13 to 41 times more, so a route that fits a frame compiled may not fit one here. The page sees one refused `new Function`, reported as one violation, and downloads a lazy chunk. | Allow `'unsafe-eval'` where the policy can take it; the fragment and point-draw tiers and the package routes that avoid the CPU tier run at full speed either way. |
| Package routes on the WebGL2 compatibility layer | Measured (Edge 156, ANGLE Direct3D 11, NVIDIA RTX A6000, 2026-10-29, frame wall time, native WebGPU then the layer; [routes-browser.mjs](../packages/webgl2-compat/test/routes-browser.mjs) checks the pixels). DOF at 1280 x 720: 0.72 ms, 10.1 ms on its route (913 ms on the generic CPU tier before). Splats: 10,000 in 1.2 ms, 17.9 ms (44 ms before); 100,000 in 2.1 ms, 39 ms (308 ms before). `CulledInstances`, 10,000 parts: 0.98 ms, 1.14 ms (11.7 ms before). Before their routes of 2026-11-05, VFX on the CPU tier: 5,000 particles 0.61 ms, 6.5 ms; 50,000 in 0.67 ms, 48 ms. Scatter on the CPU tier: 10,000 instances 0.56 ms, 5.4 ms; 100,000 in 0.85 ms, 40 ms. | WebGL2 has no workgroup memory, atomics or compute; each route does the same work another way. | The same pixels as native WebGPU. Splats read their projected keys back once a frame (a counted stall); `'occlusion'` culling culls against the frustum only (the same image, more parts drawn). XR needs the `/xr` entry; the voxel path tracer has the next row. | Budget VFX particles and scatter instances by the per-frame times measured here (about 1 µs per particle and 0.4 µs per instance on this PC). |
| Voxel path tracer on the WebGL2 compatibility layer (2026-11-06) | Checked with `browser-check.mjs --compat` on ANGLE Vulkan, RTX A6000: first hits equal `raycast`, the analytic, energy, sky, texture, shape and transparency cases pass at their usual tolerances, and captures, material tables, the denoiser and a device restore work. Firefox, Safari, ANGLE Metal and an integrated GPU have not been run. Speed, diagnostic because other jobs ran: a 1500 x 1100, 64-sample denoised render 6.4 s against 2.3 s native, a 400 x 240 viewport frame 24 ms against 9 ms. | WebGL2 has no compute, so `trace` runs in a fragment shader and a small kernel adds its sums to the image. The compile time is the shader compiler's: `D3DCompile` inlines every function and its time grows worse than linearly. | ANGLE on Direct3D 11, the default on Windows, does not finish `trace`: Microsoft's shader compiler was still running at the 50 minute limit, three times (its smaller kernels take 27 to 86 s each), while the page kept drawing at full rate. So that route shows the raster image and never a traced frame (`docs/known-bugs.md`). Frames show the raster image until then. | On a Direct3D browser keep the raster view; on such a page WebGPU is the route, where the browser has it. Closing this needs the path loop split into passes (`docs/known-bugs.md`). |
| Site demos on the WebGL2 compatibility layer | Measured by `npm run visual:compat` (part of `check:merge`): every site demo and Khronos gallery model, natively and on the layer in one browser on one GPU; the per-demo table, with each pixel difference and frame cost, is [webgl2-compat-demos.md](webgl2-compat-demos.md). | WebGL2 has no compute, storage buffers or atomics; the layer runs those kernels as fragment draws, point draws or on the CPU, and Shadows, DOF, splats and culling take routes of their own. | Each demo either draws the same pixels as WebGPU (at most 0.1% of pixels more than 2 levels apart, mean at most 0.25), runs with differences the table states, or refuses the layer for a part that needs WebGPU (MargoError `unsupported`, shown over the scene). The per-sample MSAA difference is an accepted difference (Scott accepted, 2026-10-31): WebGL2 cannot read one sample of a multisampled texture, so the layer resolves it first, and DOF, the gameplay sandbox and Night Citadel differ from WebGPU through it ([the section on it](webgl2-compat-demos.md#accepted-difference-per-sample-reads-of-multisampled-textures)); at 1x MSAA they are within the parity rule. A demo's median frame on the layer must stay under 50 ms of main-thread work, or under twice its native frame when that is slower already (`npm run visual:compat:budget`, part of `check:merge`, with the GPU held exclusively; `--budget=<ms>` sets another limit, for an integrated GPU; on an integrated GPU that run is not recorded yet; on the RTX A6000 gameplay-sandbox takes 16.7 ms). | [webgpu.html](../site/webgpu.html) explains the compatibility mode to visitors; [examples/dual-viewport.html](../examples/dual-viewport.html) draws one scene both ways with the live difference and frame times. |
| Download size of the WebGL2 compatibility layer | Measured by `npm run measure:size`'s record ([entry-sizes.json](../examples/package-benchmarks/results/entry-sizes.json), `splits`; the sizes gate holds both numbers, [lazy-boundary.test.js](../tests/lazy-boundary.test.js) holds the boundary), min+gzip, 2026-11-06. Two numbers: the base, everything `install()` loads (device, GLSL back end, WGSL front end, the render path), is 70,274 B (235,884 B minified); the lazy part is 30,819 B (96,623 B minified): the compute runtime, which only a page that makes its first compute pipeline loads, about 6,000 B of it the interpreter chunk that only a page without `unsafe-eval` downloads, and the texture read-back chunk, which a page loads with its first `copyTextureToBuffer` or compute pipeline. All of it, as one file, is 100,514 B. A page on WebGPU downloads none of it. | The layer translates WGSL to GLSL and runs WebGPU's device model on WebGL2, so the base holds a full WGSL front end (check.ts 61 KB, parser, builtins) and the GLSL emitter. Property names are not mangled. | Accepted difference (Scott accepted, 2026-10-31; the limit itself was set 2026-10-30): the sizes gate holds the base to 73,000 B, not the 60,000 B target the roadmap once stated and no gate enforced. The base carries the full WGSL to GLSL translator and the WGSL checker, both needed on the first frame; native WebGPU always validates shaders, so a production build without the checker would behave differently. The lazy part is measured and gated separately. The five largest modules of the base by their share of the gzip bytes, before read-back left device/render.ts (2,080 B, 2026-11-06): device/render.ts 14.0 KB, glsl/emit.ts 13.9 KB, wgsl/check.ts 11.4 KB, device/resources.ts 5.0 KB, wgsl/builtins.ts 4.3 KB. | A page that never runs compute or reads a texture back pays the base only. [webgl2-compat-roadmap.md](webgl2-compat-roadmap.md) tracks the size work. |
| Content Security Policy | Enforced by the browser: core compiles its Wasm kernel, so `script-src` needs `'wasm-unsafe-eval'` (as do Draco and Basis). The standard WebGPU path and the other WebGL2 tiers need no `'unsafe-eval'`, workers included (the optional WebGL2 CPU compute tier of `webgl2-compat` builds its shaders with `new Function` where the page allows it, and without `'unsafe-eval'` runs the same kernels on an interpreter, 13 to 41 times slower, a diagnostic figure): the Draco/KTX2 decoder worker loads its Emscripten code with `importScripts`, and the build replaces the `new Function` wrapper generators in the Basis glue with closures, output byte-identical ([no-eval-workers.test.js](../tests/no-eval-workers.test.js)). No COOP/COEP. Checked with zero violations on Edge, one policy on the page and every worker ([csp-check.json](../examples/package-benchmarks/results/csp-check.json)). | Wasm is how the kernel stays small and fast. | Without `'wasm-unsafe-eval'`, `Engine.create` rejects ("Compiling or instantiating WebAssembly module violates the following Content Security policy directive") ([csp-check-negative.json](../examples/package-benchmarks/results/csp-check-negative.json)). | Headers for Cloudflare Pages, Netlify, Vercel and Nginx in [deploying.md](deploying.md). A Draco or Basis build you substitute for the bundled one may still call `new Function` (three.js's Basis transcoder does) and then needs `'unsafe-eval'` for its worker. |
| React app budget (what a bundler emits and the browser loads) | Measured and enforced by the bundler matrix (`npm run modern:test:bundlers`, part of `check:merge`): a Vite 8 build of the React cube page (`Canvas`, `Mesh`, `BoxGeometry`, `PbrMaterial`, `OrbitControls` from `@margoengine/react/controls`, `useFrame`; React 19) loads 94,216 B gzip before it runs (React, React DOM, core, the stage and the React bindings; budget 100,000 B), and with `@margoengine/react` installed alone the whole build emits 104,197 B gzip of JavaScript and no Wasm (budget 115,000 B). The matrix loads the built page in Edge and fails if it requests Wasm, an optional engine package or the BVH raycaster, and builds the same page with `@margoengine/react` alone in Next.js 16 (webpack and Turbopack), Rollup and esbuild: every module resolves and no optional package is emitted. Breakdown: [React page](#react-page). The build prints no warnings, without `@vitejs/plugin-react` too: `'use client'` is only on the `react-server` entries. | `@margoengine/react`'s root holds only bindings that need no optional package; each optional one is a subpath named after its package (`/controls`, `/gltf-pro`, `/shadows`, ...), and the stage's features are entries of their own (`@margoengine/stage/gltf`, ...), so no root reaches an optional package, not even through a lazy `import()` (`tests/optional-peer-reach.test.js`). | With `npm i margoengine`, `margoengine/react` emits no Wasm either: the bindings that reach gltf-pro's decoders are separate entries, because Vite copies the Basis (527,333 B) and Draco (192,420 B) `.wasm` to `dist` when it reads those modules, before tree-shaking. | A page that imports `margoengine/react/gltf-pro`, `/fbx`, `/animation` or `/materials` has the two files in `dist`, requested only for a model that needs them. `@margoengine/react` alone, plus the packages its subpaths name, also installs less. |
| Number fields in package classes (2026-11-05) | Enforced by test ([class-fields.test.js](../tests/class-fields.test.js)): packages on the shared tsconfig build with `useDefineForClassFields: false`. `@margoengine/materials` keeps its declarations; `core`, `stage`, `kernel`, `shared`, `react` and `margoengine` have their own tsconfig and are unchanged. | A number field declared before the constructor starts as `undefined`, so V8 stores it tagged and each write of a fraction allocates. `Material.copy` lists its values with `Object.keys(new Material())`, so an optional material field must exist. | `#private` fields must be declared, so a bare one still starts as `undefined`; none was measured. 10,000 `Spring.update` calls took 0.41 ms and take 0.28 ms (Node 26, this PC, by hand, so diagnostic). | Assign a hot number field in the constructor. |
| First npm install | Measured by `npm run publish:rehearsal` against a stand-in registry; nothing is on npm yet: the 102 tarballs pack to 7,151,007 B, and `npm install margoengine` puts 23,304,020 B in `node_modules`, every package included. | A bundler reads a package only when it is imported; the disk cost of the one install is the price of having every entry at hand. | An app bundles only what it imports. | `@margoengine/<name>` installs one package and what it needs. |
| Cloth, particle fluids and soft bodies | By decision (2026-10-31): out of scope for v1.0.0. | Each is a large package of its own; `@margoengine/physics` has `Rope`. | No such API exists; the parity matrix lists them as NOT PLANNED ([threejs-parity.md](threejs-parity.md)). | GPU particles in `@margoengine/vfx`, Rapier joints in `@margoengine/physics`. |
| No visual editor | By design: Margo is code-first. | An editor is a product of its own; the engine stays a library. | Scenes are built in code or authored in another tool. | Author in Blender and export glTF or `.margo` (`@margoengine/blender`, `tools/blender-addon`, [guides/blender-game.md](guides/blender-game.md)); inspect at runtime with `@margoengine/inspector` and its `/frame` debugger; move objects with `@margoengine/controls`'s TransformControls. A Margo Studio editor is planned after 1.0. |
| Bricked volumes | Enforced by the budget ([bricks.ts](../packages/sciviz/src/bricks.ts), [bricks.test.js](../packages/sciviz/tests/bricks.test.js)): `@margoengine/sciviz/bricks` holds at most `memoryBudget` bytes of GPU memory (default 512 MiB) and marches at most 2048 samples per ray. Measured at 4096 x 1024 x 1024 voxels (8.6 GiB as half floats, past the 2048 texture limit), 1280 x 720, RTX A6000, default budget: median 4.3-12.6 ms per frame over four views. Drawn against the same volume as one texture: largest channel difference 2 of 255. | A single 3D texture stops at the GPU's limit and costs all its bytes up front. | Past the budget, finer bricks wait and coarser ones draw; between levels values can step by what averaging removed. The page table takes one texel per full-resolution brick and must fit the 3D limit. Errors: `RangeError` naming `memoryBudget` or `brickSize`; `MargoError` code `failed` for a read that failed. | Raise `memoryBudget` or lower `detail` for more detail; frame time grows with the bricks drawn (2 GiB, closest view: median 653 ms). |
| Sciviz NRRD volumes (`@margoengine/sciviz/nrrd`, 2026-11-07) | By the reader: everything `@margoengine/loaders/nrrd` reads (raw, ascii, hex, gzip, bzip2 with `@margoengine/decoders`), at most `maxSamples` (default 536,870,912) samples; one frame and one component (or the magnitude) per layer ([nrrd.ts](../packages/sciviz/src/nrrd.ts), [nrrd.test.js](../packages/sciviz/tests/nrrd.test.js)). | A layer draws one scalar grid; picking a component or the magnitude makes a `Float32Array` copy. | The volume draws axis-aligned in index order: the file's rotation and origin (`volume.matrix`) are not applied; a series draws one frame at a time. Errors: the loader's coded `LoaderError`s, `RangeError` for a frame or component outside the file or a failed fetch. | Apply `volume.matrix` to other data to line it up; use `fig.timeline` with `nrrdGrid(volume, { frame })` for a series. |
| Sciviz medical and array formats (`@margoengine/sciviz/formats`, 2026-11-07) | By the readers ([formats.ts](../packages/sciviz/src/formats.ts), [formats.test.js](../packages/sciviz/tests/formats.test.js)): NIfTI datatypes 2, 4, 8, 16, 64, 128, 256, 512, 768, 1024, 1280 and 2304; DICOM uncompressed pixel data of 8, 16 or 32 bits; Zarr regular chunks with the bytes, gzip, zlib, crc32c and transpose codecs. At most `maxSamples` (default 536,870,912) samples per file or read. | Every reader decodes on the main thread with the shared inflater (`packages/shared/inflate.js`), which reports where a stream ends (the DICOM deflate pad byte, gzip members); 64-bit integers become `Float64Array`, exact up to 2^53. JSON cannot tell `1.0` from `1`, so a Zarr integer fill value written with a fraction part is taken as the integer. | Refused with code `unsupported`, naming what: NIfTI complex and 128-bit types; DICOM compressed transfer syntaxes (JPEG, JPEG-LS, JPEG 2000, RLE), 1-bit pixels, YBR_PARTIAL_422 and 420, odd-width YBR_FULL_422, 32-bit OW pixel data in explicit VR big endian (retired PS3.5 A.3 leaves its word order open), and multi-frame images whose Frame Increment Pointer is not Frame Time or Frame Time Vector (frames that step through space); Zarr blosc, zstd, lz4, sharding, filters, storage transformers and unknown extensions without `must_understand: false`. A file that has both a Modality LUT Sequence and rescale values is a `RangeError` (PS3.3 C.11.1 allows one). A series must share one size and SeriesInstanceUID. The volume draws axis-aligned in index order; `matrix` gives the voxel to world or patient transform for other data. `RangeError` for a damaged file or a box outside the array. | Decode compressed DICOM pixels with your own codec and pass the slices to `dicomSeries` as `DICOMImage` objects; recompress Zarr with gzip or zlib. |
| Sciviz unstructured grids (`@margoengine/sciviz/grids`, 2026-11-08) | Linear tetrahedra (10), hexahedra (12), wedges (13) and pyramids (14); `parseVtk` also reads voxels (11) as hexahedra. One value per point or per cell. | Surface and slice are built on the CPU each call; a moved slice is a new `sliceGrid` call and `layer.update(set)`. Cells are taken as convex with planar faces; a twisted hexahedron face is split along its 0-2 diagonal. | `parseVtk` reads legacy files up to version 4.2 (CELLS with a count before each cell); version 5.1 files (OFFSETS and CONNECTIVITY), XML `.vtu`, binary `long` data and other DATASET kinds are refused naming what. Other cell types (vertices, lines, surface and quadratic cells) are skipped and counted in `skipped`. Wedges follow VTK's corrected order (0-1-2 counter-clockwise seen from 3-4-5); a file written in the older drawn order shows those wedges inside out. | Convert 5.1 or `.vtu` files to legacy 4.2 in ParaView (File, Save Data, legacy VTK), or pass the arrays to the grid functions directly. |
| Sciviz graph layouts (`@margoengine/sciviz/graph`, 2026-11-08) | Any node count the memory holds; edges up to the GPU's `maxStorageBufferBindingSize` / 8 per layer (16,777,216 at the 128 MiB default). | The layout runs on the main thread, one iteration per `step()`, about E + N log N work; `run()` yields between iterations. No speed is claimed. | Undirected straight edges; weights scale the pull. No frame walls (a pull toward the origin instead), no edge-crossing or label placement. Like any force layout it can settle in a local minimum; ten 24-node ring seeds at the default theta 0.9 settle with edges 3 % to 6 % uneven and no crossing. The tree opens cells by Barnes's 1994 test (d > side / theta + the centre of mass's offset), not the 1986 one, which kept one ring seed in ten (seed 3) crossed with edges 13 % uneven: on a 20,000-node graph that opens 10 % more cells on the first step in 2D (19 % in 3D) and 21 % (41 %) over the next 100 steps, counted. The layer frames the positions it was made with unless `bounds` is given. Edges are one-pixel lines. | Run the layout in a worker and send `positions` to `layer.setPositions`; pass `bounds` for a layout that will grow. |
| Sciviz geographic layers (`@margoengine/sciviz/geo`, 2026-11-08) | Zoom 0 to 24; up to `maxTiles` tiles per layer (default 256), all loaded at one zoom when the layer is made. | Each tile is a 16 by 16 grid projected in the vertex shader; a 256-tile layer is 256 draws. | Raster tiles only (PNG, JPEG or anything `createImageBitmap` reads), one zoom per layer, no level-of-detail streaming as the camera moves. Mercator ends at about 85.05 degrees; the globe is the WGS 84 ellipsoid with heights along its normal. A 404 tile counts in `missing` and stays empty; other HTTP errors reject. No vector tiles, no datum shifts. | Make a new layer at a higher zoom for a smaller `area`; use `@margoengine/terrain` for heights from tiles. |
| Light-tier frame rate | Enforced ([index.ts](../packages/quality/src/index.ts)): `paceEngine` holds the light tier to an even rate of at least 60 FPS: 60 on 60, 120 and 240 Hz, 72 on 144 Hz, 83 on 165 Hz, 67 on 200 Hz, and every refresh of 75, 90 and 100 Hz. | Each frame spans a whole number of refreshes, so the rate cannot land on exactly 60 on every display, and an uneven 60 on 100 Hz would judder. | A 100 Hz display renders 100 FPS on the light tier, which asks twice the GPU work of the 50 FPS it used to get; a GPU that cannot keep up misses refreshes as it would on any uncapped tier. Not yet seen on a physical panel at any of these rates: the numbers come from a stand-in clock, a real page on a simulated 100 Hz clock and a fake clock in the unit test. | `capFrameRate(engine, rate)` sets another rate; `?quality=high` is not paced. |
| Supersampled stills (`@margoengine/quality/ssaa`, 2026-11-06) | Enforced ([ssaa.ts](../packages/quality/src/ssaa.ts)): 1 to 1024 frames, picture size up to the device's `maxTextureDimension2D`. Each sample is a full render, so cost grows with `samples`. Two `rgba32float` sums, the frame and image textures and a read buffer live during a still (44 bytes a pixel, 365 MB at 3840 x 2160). The picture is opaque 8-bit display colour: no alpha, no HDR. A size other than the canvas makes effects that size themselves to the frame rebuild their textures, twice per still, and core keeps its 4x target for that size until the next one. History effects soften a still: with `TAA` attached a 16-sample still reads 27 dB against the same still without it. `TAAUpscale` throws, as it does with any view. | Each sample is a full render, so cost grows with `samples`. | With `TAA` attached a 16-sample still reads 27 dB against the same still without it, and `TAAUpscale` throws. | Dispose `TAA` (and any `TAAUpscale`) before the still and attach it again after, and lower `samples` to cut the cost. |
| Feature scaling (`@margoengine/quality/features`, 2026-11-06) | A step that saved under 3% of the frame rests 120 decisions, about a minute at 60 FPS. | A saving is the frame cost before minus after one step, over one 30-frame window: anything else that moves the cost in that window counts toward it, and a measured value is averaged with the last, so it corrects over time. | A step of shadow `resolution` or `cascades` or of the fog `grid` allocates textures, one hitch each. It lowers nothing when every knob is at its floor or resting. | Leave a target out of `featureKnobs` (for example `shadows`) to keep it fixed, or write your own `FeatureKnob` (see `optionKnob`) with a `weight`. Pair a lowered `camera.far` with fog. |
| Battery and heat (`@margoengine/quality/power`, 2026-11-06) | Heat takes about half a minute to show at 60 FPS, longer at lower rates. | Not measured on a machine really throttling: `FrameDrift` is proved on modelled frame costs. Battery status is Chromium only, and CPU pressure is Chromium only and says nothing of the GPU; elsewhere frame times are the only source, so a machine on battery stays at level 0. | A scene that grows heavier gradually, with no step above 15% between readings, reads as heat. A full-tier page keeps its 4x multisampling until its next load. | Call `restart()` on the watch after a change you made, so growth you caused is not read as heat, and raise `rise` if it fires too soon. On the full tier pass `reload: () => location.reload()` to `watchPower` to move to the light tier at once. |

The sort that writes parents first is an insertion sort, which costs most with many nested bodies in reverse order: not measured. | Parents first is what makes a child's local pose right in the same frame. | Measured speed with thousands of nested bodies. | Put bodies on root meshes, or on no mesh (`addBody(null, options)`), where you can: only bodies on child meshes are sorted parents first. |
