Substeps run from 1 to 65,535. | By method: the integrator's own error against a parabola is `g h t / 2`, so it halves when substeps double; a dynamic body with no mass does not fall, and additional mass is spread as the colliders are, so it scales the inertia with the mass. Shapes today: ball, box, capsule, each with a closed-form mass, and hull. Enforced: a hull is a closed convex polyhedron with flat faces of at most 256 edges and no two neighbours that are one plane split in two, within about 0.8 degrees with every corner within half of FLAT, 1e-4 of the hull's size, of the other's plane (a mesh with such faces loses contact points, Gregorius 2015, so `addHull` refuses it); a real crease under 0.8 degrees between faces too wide to be one flat face is taken, as a hull of random points has them; its rows last for the world's life. By method: the separating axis test is linear in vertices per face and quadratic in edges, with no hill climbing or cached axis yet, so a hull of hundreds of faces costs a step more than it should until the quiet pass. By method: GJK stops after 64 descents, and the expanding polytope holds 96 vertices and 192 faces; a depth search that runs out gives no contact for that step and the next step tries again. By method: a point with a new id continues an old one within 2 cm on the first body, so a contact that jumps further starts its impulses from zero. By method: a contact's normal is found once a step and held for its substeps, and a point the body has not yet reached acts as a plane (Catto 2013's speculative point), so a fast body passing a corner can feel a ghost of that plane; not measured, batch 126 owns it. Contact defaults: 8 substeps, 2 passes, 120 Hz capped at a quarter of the substep rate, damping ratio 10, slop 0.2 mm, push speed 3 m/s. By method (2026-11-08): at one pass a substep a contact between two dynamic bodies that neither dominates is capped at an eighth of the substep rate (30 Hz at 4 substeps) while it carries under 50 times the lighter body's weight, as one pass settles no pile at a quarter; the ground, kinematic and dominant bodies and heavier loads keep the quarter. Measured at 4 substeps of one pass: the 20-row pyramid sleeps at 2.3 s, its top moved 32 mm, its bottom row 1.19 mm into the floor; 30 rows, whose bottom pairs carry about 40 times a cube's weight, sleep at 2.0 s and rest at 5.9e-4 m/s with sleeping off; the 1:100 and 1:1000 stacks keep the quarter and sag 14.4 and 137 mm. A deeper pile whose bottom pairs pass 50 times takes the quarter there; not measured past 30 rows. By method (2026-11-07): a substep relaxes as many times as it pushes, so 2 relaxing passes of joints and contacts at the defaults where there was 1: one pass left the friction anchors' push in a pile of 14 rows or more as speed, and it rocked for good. By method: a manifold's normal rows sweep until no impulse moves by more than rounding, at most 16 sweeps a pass, on the points' speeds alone; a relaxing pass settles in 3 to 8 sweeps on the 1:1000 stack. By method (2026-11-08): a manifold of four points ends each sweep with the exact step along the one pattern of its impulses that moves no body, the points' shares of one load among themselves, which the sweeps settled only by the leak (about 3 percent a sweep), so a soft pass ran all 16; it now settles in about 4 (perf-history 80). Friction keeps one sweep. By method (2026-11-08): inside the slop a biased pass closes a point rigidly to the slop's depth, (gap + slop) / h as a speculative row (Catto's Solver2D soft step), and the soft push acts only past it, so every point of a resting manifold rests at the same depth whatever its load: a cube released on a slope below tan t = mu holds under 1e-6 m from its first second (it moved up to 3.8e-5 m as its points sank at speeds that followed their loads). Measured on the harness, defaults and 4 substeps of one pass: creep 5.5e-5 and 5.9e-6 m to 0; the 1:1000 stack's top drifts 1.6e-8 and 1.4e-7 m (6.6e-7 and 3.7e-6 m before the exact step of the four points' shares); the 20-row pyramid at the defaults sleeps at 1.50 s (1.78 s before that step, 3.2 s earlier), and at 4 substeps of one pass, which fails its bars either way, moved its top 0.291 m (0.277) and its fastest box at 30 s 0.51 m/s (0.18); with the exact step of the four points' shares (2026-11-08) 0.270 m and 0.28 m/s, still awake until the eighth-rate spring of two movable bodies at one pass (above) let it sleep. By method (2026-11-08): a ball or a capsule meets the other surface at its core plus its radius along the normal, wherever it has turned to, so a rolling ball's point stays on the floor; its lever arm stays the step's contact point. A ball from rest at 30 degrees rolls within 1e-5 of 5/7 g sin t (it ran 1.0033 in its first second). By method (2026-11-08): a point struck while its gap is still open notes the speed it will meet the surface with, v^2 = v0^2 + 2 a d over the gap, gravity's gain added, so a ball from 1 m at e 0.5 peaks at 0.2487 m at the defaults and 0.2473 m at 4 substeps of one pass (was 0.2481 and 0.2432). By method: a resting contact sags by its load over its stiffness, which follows the lighter body, so a heavy box on a light one sits deeper: 3.9 mm at 1:100 and 3.5 cm at 1:1000 with metre cubes. A rolling ball slips 0.35 percent of its speed by 2.5 s, as friction is a stiff spring, and lands 0.13 percent over 5/7 g sin t. Enforced: a manifold holds four points. By method: the reduction always keeps the deepest point, so its area can fall short of the best four: 0.711 of the best at worst and 0.9915 on average over 3,000 random sets. Enforced: 536,870,912 pair rows and 32-bit offsets inside the block; a step throws when memory cannot hold the pairs. By method: pairs are found once a step over the motion expected at its start, so a body a contact turns round mid-step can leave its fat box until the next step; the margin is 0.04 m at any body size; a fixed body written by hand needs `touch`; a collider's row comes back only after a step. A single add or removal costs one pass over the lists, so adding one collider a frame to a very large world pays for the whole list. Until the narrow phase lands, two bodies touch when their fat boxes overlap, so islands run larger than they will. Writing a sleeper's columns does not wake it. A body at a steady pose with its energy under the threshold can go to sleep mid-air if nothing pushes it, as gravity is not a force the sleep test sees: a body at rest under gravity moves, so this needs zero gravity or `gravityScale` 0. A snapshot is the whole block, so its size follows capacity, not the bodies in use. By method: f32 state, as a far world needs `FloatingOrigin`. Needs Wasm SIMD, as core's kernel already does, so there is no second blueprint. `Physics`, `PhysicsBody` and `PhysicsCollider` of the entry serve free motion only: `setType`, `follow`, friction and the other contact settings, joints, queries, character controllers and `addModel` throw `unsupported` until their batch. `body.sleep()` and impulses take effect at the next step. A snapshot reads only in the kernel build that wrote it, and a `Physics`-level restore waits for `/snapshot`. Measured (LIM-366): 10,000 steps hash-equal in Node, native x86-64, Chromium 156 and Firefox 157 on one Windows PC; Safari, WebKit, ARM and any other machine are unmeasured. On that PC Windows will not start Playwright's Firefox from the home folder (a side-by-side error on `mozglue`, as Chromium's full `chrome.exe` gives there), so the run used a copy of it under the worktree through `REPLAY_FIREFOX`. | The kernel is staged behind `/next` until the switch; the root entry still runs Rapier. Bit-equal results hold for one kernel build, not across kernel versions. | Use the root entry, which still runs Rapier. |
| Own physics joints (`@margoengine/physics/next`, preview, 2026-11-07) | By method: locks are exact along a path of joints, so a chain of 50 hung straight holds a 1000:1 end mass to under 1e-5 of its length, at rest and swung at 3 m/s. By method: a lock's turning arm is read at the start of a substep, so a light link under a large pull turns as an explicit spring of stiffness about twice the pull times its arm; past about two radians a substep of that turn it grows. A chain of 50 laid out level with a 1000:1 end mass passes 1e-4 of its length at step 32 and 2 times by step 60 with 1 kg links of a sphere's inertia (0.001 kg m2 at 0.05 m arms); 16 substeps hold it to step 66, 32 to step 229, ten times the inertia to step 219 (`docs/known-bugs.md`). Where a branch or a ring ends a path, paths meet pass by pass: a grid under its own weight opens 0.18 mm at 8 by 8 and 0.95 mm at 30 by 30, a heavy hub with three arms 7.3 mm, and 5.3 to 8.7 mm with the hub started a micrometre to 0.3 mm aside, as the widest gap is the peak of a swing that rounding steers, but 1000 kg on the corner of an 8 by 8 grid of 1 kg links opens 69 mm and still moves at 0.2 m/s after 10 s (8 passes: 13 mm). By method: limits are one row at a time, so a long run of hinges on their stops carries its moment slowly: six links droop 48 mm, twelve droop 0.91 m and still move at 0.29 m/s in the last 5 s of 10. By method: a limit past its edge pushes back with the mass its row sees, as a contact does, so a limb a floor holds past its cone rests there: a hand of 0.36 kg comes to 0.02 rad past its cone, one of 3.6 g stays 0.49 rad past it under its 1.5 kg arm. At the edge a limit holds any load: a link of a hundredth of the mass carries a full one 0.008 rad past a cone of 0.2. By method: a rope is one row at a time, so two ropes under a 1000:1 end mass rest 35 mm long, 1.7 percent, and stay there. By method: a spring is implicit Euler, so with no damping one of 5 rad/s loses 23 percent of its amplitude in 10 s. `/articulation` (batch 128) is where branches and limits go exact. By method: one walk over every joint row a step, and rows and factors refreshed twice a substep, which the quiet pass may halve. By design: 2 passes at least for joints, so `iterations: 1` adds a settling pass for them; `generic` is not served yet. By design: a sensor reads the pose a step starts from, so an event comes up to a step after the overlap; two fixed bodies never meet, sensor or not; a disabled collider keeps its mass; dominance acts in contacts, not in joints. By method, four limits on the lock solve, each with a test that fails without it. (1) A row with under n u of its size left after the rows before it depends on them and carries nothing, n the rows of its path and u f32's epsilon (Higham 1990, stop 4.3 at his n u), read off Householder QR of M^-1/2 J^T so the doubles round each row only by its own length: a body held by ten rows on six freedoms stands still where it used to reach 18,904,462 m/s, and a path with 13 rows on 12 freedoms no longer keeps its dependent row (4e-7 of its size by elimination of J M^-1 J^T, 1e-32 now). Cost: a row within about sqrt(n u) radians of depending, 1.3e-3 rad on a path of 14 rows, is dropped too; and the kernel grew 4,449 B deflated (125,328 to 129,777 B). (2) A lock closes a gap at 3 m/s plus what the turning carries its anchors, the clamp of Buss and Kim 2005, whose D_max,i = d_i + D_max this is. A valid scene reaches it when a body is moved by hand off its joint or a hub turns at 8 rad/s at 100 m. Pairs are found once a step, so without it a joint 6 m open jumps a body past a wall in one substep. Cost: a wide gap takes more steps, and on the proof scenes it acted on 5 rows of 6,960 in the limb and moved no figure. (3) Where rows nearly depend on each other a push is a Gauss-Newton step outside its model, so a body's turn may rise by at most 0.2 rad a substep times N / M, and its centre by that much of its arm, N what the path asks and M what its bodies would each move (Buss and Kim 2005, section 4; they use 0.79 rad for inverse kinematics, here 0.79 lets a taut chain reach 39 m/s and 0.1 starts to cut the chain of fifty). A valid scene reaches it when a chain is pulled straight between two points further apart than it is long, the same pose as an arm reaching past its reach. Cost: what is cut stays open into the next substeps, and the whole path's push is cut, not only its bad direction, as a path has no singular value decomposition. It cut nothing in any scene of `kernel/tests/joints.rs`. (4) A warm start that would add more than a push's energy, half the island's mass times 3 m/s squared, is taken only as far as the minimum of the quadratic the passes descend. A valid scene reaches it when a limit catches a swing, as what the row holds is an impact and no load: once per catch on the proof scenes, keeping 0 to 7 percent. Cost: that row starts its substep cold, and no figure in `kernel/tests/joints.rs` moved. No paper does this; the line search is standard, its use here is our own. By method: a lock is exact and a contact is a capped spring, so a joint stretched through a wall drags its body through at about 1.5 m/s. By method: a turn past about 1 rad a substep leaves rows nothing to read, so a lock on a hub at 77 rad/s holds with 2 substeps, to 13 m on a radius of 10, and with 1 substep runs away and is refused after 59 steps. By design: a body past 1e15 in position or speed stops the world with `failed`, checked at the end of a step: a run away faster than that within one step would get past it, not seen in 200 random webs of 10,000 steps. Their top, 1.3e8 m/s for one step, is a kinematic turn of 77 rad/s forcing locks near a toggle, where the exact speed is one over the angle: only the pushing pass's bias is cut, after its speeds are solved in full, so a speed is answered in full. Scenes with no answer are only kept in the numbers: 60 random webs of unsatisfiable joints stay under 1e5 m/s, seed 45 spins under the 18,400 rad/s its kinematic turns can carry (2,559 rad/s; a cut of the whole solve once gave 162,278), and which seed is fastest changes with any constant; seeds 1 and 35 spin under 18,400 rad/s and hold every joint under the 56.85 m the scene's cube allows (956 rad/s and 38.0 m to step 400, 1,354 rad/s and 40.9 m to step 600), but near a toggle a row that does not yet depend is answered in full, so seed 49 reaches 10,991 m/s and a joint 165.8 m open and the 60 webs have no gap bar yet (`docs/known-bugs.md`). By method: each joint of the chain of 50 hung straight opens under 2e-5 m at rest and swung, under the same 1e-4 of its length as its stretch; laid out level and let fall, a joint opens 7.0 mm at 1:1, 9.7 mm at 10:1, 23 mm at 100:1 and 0.38 to 0.45 m at 1000:1 within 20 s, from rows that turn within a substep (`docs/known-bugs.md`) | A joint in a branch, a loop or on its limits settles over passes, so its gap under a far heavier load is the price; a chain is exact. | A grid or ring of joints under a far heavier load opens a gap (0.95 mm at 30 by 30; 69 mm for 1000 kg on the corner of an 8 by 8 grid), and a long run of hinges on their stops droops. | Use a chain, which is exact. Set `iterations` to 8, which narrows the 69 mm gap to 13 mm. An `/articulation` entry (batch 128) is planned and does not exist yet. |
| Own physics convex hulls (`@margoengine/physics/next`, preview, 2026-11-07) | By method: Quickhull with fat planes, a point within 2^-20 of the cloud's size of the hull counting as on it, then triangles within a quarter of FLAT, 2.5e-5 of the size, of a face's supporting plane merge into one face. Enforced: a face holds 256 corners at most, so the hull of a 257-sided prism is refused with the fix. Enforced: fewer than four points, or points on one line or in one plane, are refused with a `RangeError` naming the fix. | A hull may stand 2.5e-5 of its size off its points, so faces stay large and few. | So a point may lie up to that far outside the hull, worst measured 2.0e-5 over 85 test clouds, and a point that close to a face is dropped: 10,000 points on a sphere keep 5,569 corners. By design: hull rows last for the world's life, so colliders built from the same points share one row and a game that builds new hulls each frame grows the world. Diagnostic, not a benchmark: 10,000 sphere points build in about 73 ms in Node on the dev PC while other lanes ran; batch 129 measures it against Rapier. Faces that meet under 0.8 degrees and are too wide to merge stay two, which the kernel takes. | Build a hull once (`convexHull`, then `addHull`) and share its row between colliders, rather than building new hulls each frame. |
| Own physics triangle meshes (`@margoengine/physics/next`, preview, 2026-11-07) (LIM-518) | By design: a triangle mesh has no volume, so it gives a body no mass and refuses `mass` and `density`, as a Rapier trimesh gives none; both sides of a triangle collide, so a shape pushed past a triangle's plane is pushed on through it. Not served yet: a mesh against a mesh gives no contacts (Rapier meets composite against composite; batch 139.3). By method: one manifold of up to four points for each triangle a shape touches, each a pair row of its own, so a box across a crease holds both normals and a large box on a fine mesh solves a manifold per triangle under it. By method: `fixInternalEdges` (default false, as in Rapier) sorts an edge as convex, concave or flat from the triangle across it, joined by corner position so texture seams still join, an edge of three triangles staying open; a fold under 1e-3 (about 0.06 degrees) is flat. Measured with it on the physics-refs harness inputs (Node, dev PC, 8 substeps, 2 passes): a ball and a box slide 8 s over a flat 127 m grid with velocity variance 4.3e-11 and 1.0e-12 (bar 1e-4; without it 0.34 and 0.35, the box spinning at 3.8 rad/s); 1,000 boxes on the 130,050-triangle St Helens mesh: none below the surface, no NaN; the 11 broken Thingi10K meshes all load and stay finite. Loss to fix (batch 129): on that mesh the build takes 65 ms without the edge rule (105 ms with it) where Rapier 0.21 takes 43 ms, and a step of the 1,000 boxes 1.52 ms median, 7.5 ms p95 where Rapier takes 1.14 and 4.8, both measured on a loaded machine; the tree's leaves hold two triangles and every node bins 16 ways. By design: mesh rows last for the world's life. | A manifold a triangle costs solver rows where a merged manifold would cost fewer, and keeps every face's normal | On the 130,050-triangle St Helens mesh the build takes longer than Rapier 0.21, as the row measures. | Turn on `fixInternalEdges` for sliding over a flat mesh. |
| Own physics height fields (`@margoengine/physics/next`, preview, 2026-11-07) (LIM-519) | Enforced: at least one cell each way and under 2^31 cells, each column at one x and each row at one z, growing (`MESH_BAD_GRID` otherwise). | Points rather than heights let a field share the mesh code bit for bit, and its build needs no tree | By design: laid out as Rapier's `heightfield` (rows along z, columns along x, heights column major, `scale` the whole size, centred on the body), each cell split from row r + 1, column c to row r, column c + 1 as Rapier splits it; 300 rays at Rapier 0.21's field meet the own surface within 2.2e-6 m. By design: no mass, both sides collide, `fixInternalEdges` as on a trimesh, default false. Not served yet: a field against a mesh or a field gives no contacts, as a mesh against a mesh (batch 139.3); Rapier's per-cell `HeightFieldCellStatus` (removed cells, zigzag split) is not offered, as its JS API does not offer it either (139.3). By method: the kernel keeps the grid's points, 12 bytes each where Rapier keeps a 4-byte height, and no tree: the cells under a shape's fat box are found by halving each axis, then cells whose corner heights miss the box are skipped, so a tall box over a fine field visits every cell under it. Diagnostic, on the physics-refs inputs in Node on a loaded dev PC, not benchmarks (batch 129 measures): a ball and a box slide 8 s over a flat 127 m field with velocity variance 4.0e-11 and 4.5e-13 (bar 1e-4; Rapier's harness run, without the flag, gives 0.63 and 0.099); 1,000 balls on St Helens 256 (65,025 cells): none below the surface, no NaN, a step 2.58 ms median and 2.88 ms p95 where Rapier took 2.77 and 4.31; the field builds in 2.3 ms (Rapier 2.4). Loss to check (batch 129): the 499,849-cell St Helens field builds in 16.3 ms where Rapier took 8.6, as the kernel writes and checks three numbers a point. A heightfield ramp: a ball rolls 1.0925 m in a second where Rapier's rolls 1.0974 (0.45 percent less; `tests/next-heightfield.test.js`). By design: rows last for the world's life. | Use a coarser field (fewer `rows` and `columns`) where the shape allows, and keep shapes over it small. |
| Own physics compound colliders (`@margoengine/physics/next`, preview, 2026-11-07) (LIM-520) | Not served yet: `cylinder` and `cone` parts (they throw `unsupported` until the cylinder and cone job), and a compound inside a compound, which Rapier refuses too. | By design: Rapier's `compound`, one collider of convex parts (box, sphere, capsule, convex hull) each placed in the collider's frame: one handle, one pair and one set of events against each other collider, one density for every part. Its mass, centre and principal moments match Rapier 0.21's for a five-part compound within 1e-5 (moments within 1e-4 of the largest), and are the same bits as the parts as colliders of their own (`tests/next-compound.test.js`). A dumbbell rests on its balls where Rapier's does, within 0.01 m. Child rows reuse every convex routine and the mesh code unchanged | By method: each part is a collider row of its own off the broad phase, with its own fat box written each step its body moves, and a compound pair holds a part row for each child, child pair or child and triangle that touches; children are tested one by one against the other side's box, with no tree over them, so a pair costs one box test per child each step (Rapier keeps a tree over a compound's parts; loss to measure in batch 129 for compounds of many parts, such as a decomposition). | Use fewer parts in a compound, and give parts that lie far apart colliders of their own. |
| Own physics voxel colliders (`@margoengine/physics/next`, preview, 2026-11-08) (LIM-521) | By design: Rapier's `voxels` with `setVoxel`, `combineVoxelStates` and `propagateVoxelChange`, the same names and refusals; a grid has its cells' mass and meets convex shapes, compounds and grids, and falls through triangle meshes and heightfields, as Rapier's does (seen from outside). Its rest height, mass, centre and moments match Rapier 0.21 within 0.01 m and 1e-5 (`tests/next-voxels.test.js`). Enforced: at most 2^30 cells in a grid's box, cell numbers in 32 bits. By method: one byte a cell over the box of the filled cells (a sparse grid costs its box; a grown grid gets new rows and keeps the old ones for the world's life, as meshes do); each touching cell is a part with up to four points, so a box over four cells holds four manifolds; a contact normal that leans toward a filled neighbour is snapped to the cell's nearest exposed face (Catto 2020), so a body slides across one grid's cells and across coupled grids without catching, and an uncoupled border catches as on Rapier; a dynamic grid's mass follows an edit at once (Rapier's at the next step); a pair walks the cells under the other shape's box, with no tree. | A grid costs one byte a cell over the box of the filled cells, and each touching cell is a part with up to four points. | Rays walk the cells the ray crosses; shape casts on grids are not served yet (the queries job). | Split large worlds into chunked grids and couple them; keep edits inside a grid's box to avoid growth |
| Own physics rays (`@margoengine/physics/next`, preview, 2026-11-08) (LIM-522) | By design: Rapier's `castRay`, `castRayAndGetNormal` and `intersectionsWithRay` as `raycast` and `raycastAll`, with every `QueryFilter` option and flag; rays see the poses of the last step, as Rapier's do. Measured against Rapier 0.21 on 1,200 random rays over every shape (`tests/next-queries.test.js`): the same hits within 1e-4 m and normals within 1e-3, except two rays across a height field that Rapier misses and its own ray from the surface confirms. By method: meshes use their tree, height fields and voxel grids walk the cells the ray crosses; `raycastAll` gives one hit a collider; a solid start inside a shape hits at 0 with a zero normal (Rapier gives a ball the ray reversed). | A ray costs time in proportion to the colliders in the world: the world ray runs each collider's own test in body order, with no tree over colliders (Rapier keeps one). | Many rays in a big world are slower than on Rapier until the tree over colliders lands (batch 129). | Give the query a filter (`groups`, `onlyDynamic`) or keep worlds small |
| Own physics shape queries (`@margoengine/physics/next`, preview, 2026-11-08) (LIM-494) | By design: Rapier's `castShape`, `projectPoint`, `intersectionsWithPoint` and `intersectionsWithShape` as `shapeCast`, `projectPoint`, `intersectPoint` and `intersectShape`, with every `ShapeCastOptions` option (`maxDistance`, `targetDistance`, `stopAtPenetration`, `computeImpactGeometryOnPenetration`) and every `QueryFilter` option; queries see the poses of the last step. The query shape is a ball, box, capsule or hull; a trimesh, height field, voxel grid or compound as the query throws `unsupported`. Measured (`tests/next-shape-queries.test.js`, 400 casts, 400 projections and 1,200 overlap drops on a scene of every shape): every own cast that differs from Rapier 0.21 by more than 1e-4 m is held to an exact f64 reference (the Minkowski difference of convex pieces, clipped or advanced) within 5e-5 m, and every own cast point lies on both shapes within 1e-5 m; projections match Rapier within 1e-4 m, the hull's within 1e-5 m of its f64 projection; point overlaps match Rapier exactly. Rapier's own faults found on the way (for batch 129): its casts stop short by up to about 1e-3 of their length (15 of 236 hits, including 2e-4 m on a height field even on a 1 cm cast), its cast point is off the query shape on 4 of 236, its hull projections stray up to 1e-3 m along the surface, and its `intersectionsWithShape` never finds a height field overlap with a box, capsule or hull (17 of 30 in the test; a ball is found). By method: GJK ray casting (van den Bergen 2004) sharpened by his closest-point steps from a lower bound; meshes through their tree, height fields over the cells under the swept box, voxel grids cell by cell. | A query costs time in proportion to the colliders in the world: each collider is tested in turn, with no tree over colliders (Rapier keeps one). A voxel projection reads every cell of the grid's box. | Many queries in a big world, or projections onto big voxel grids, are slower than on Rapier until the tree over colliders lands (batch 129). | Give the query a filter (`groups`, `onlyDynamic`, `predicate`) or keep worlds small |
| Own physics continuous collision (`@margoengine/physics/next`, preview, 2026-11-08) (LIM-506) | By design: Rapier's `ccd_enabled` as `ccd` on `addBody` and `body.ccd`, and `is_ccd_active` as `body.ccdActive`. After each step a dynamic body with `ccd` that moved further than its thinnest piece is thick is swept from the pose the step began at to the pose it ended at, against every collider whose fat box meets its path's box, and put back to its first touch with its velocity kept; the next step's contacts then stop it. Stopped on a body that moves and runs no sweep of its own (a kinematic body, or a dynamic one without `ccd`), it rides with that body for the rest of the step, and a moving body that comes at it after it stopped carries it the same way. A surface the body already touches at the start of a step holds it no deeper than half its thickness. A slow body, or one with `ccd` off, moves exactly as before (the same bits), and so does a fast one sliding on a floor. Rapier's `min_ccd_dt`, `max_ccd_substeps`, `soft_ccd_prediction` and CCD sensor events are not served yet (batch 140.5). Measured (`tests/next-ccd.test.js`): 1,000 bullets at 300 m/s turning at up to 50 rad/s, as 2 cm balls, boxes and capsules, at a 2 cm box wall, a two-triangle mesh and a height field stood on end: with `ccd` no bullet's centre reaches the wall's middle plane; without it boxes pass; 200 spinning bullets at a 2 cm kinematic wall coming at 20 m/s, and at a free dynamic plate, never end a step past its middle. Rapier 0.21 with `ccd` on, as a black box through the root entry, on the same 1,000 shots: 974 balls, 189 boxes and 2 capsules end a step with their centre past the far face of the 2 cm box wall (they stop at the touch, then the next step carries them into the wall and its contacts push them out on the far side); 4 boxes pass the two-triangle mesh; none pass the height field. By method: conservative advancement (Mirtich 1996, as Catto sets it out in 2013) on a lower bound of the gap from GJK's closest points and support planes. | The sweep tests every collider's fat box against the path's box, with no tree over colliders, so a CCD body costs time in proportion to the colliders in the world; a body is put back to its first touch, so it loses the rest of that step's motion. Two CCD bodies that meet each stop at the touch, and neither carries the other within the step. | Many fast CCD bodies in a big world are slower than on Rapier until the tree over colliders lands (batch 129). | Turn `ccd` on only for bodies that move further than they are thick in a step |
| Own physics cylinders and cones (`@margoengine/physics/next`, preview, 2026-11-08) (LIM-512) | By design: Rapier's `cylinder`, `cone`, `roundCylinder` and `roundCone` as `cylinder` and `cone` with `radius`, `halfHeight` (along local Y, a cone's apex at +`halfHeight`) and `borderRadius` (default 0), as colliders and as compound parts, with rays, shape casts and point queries on them. Enforced: `radius` and `halfHeight` above 0 and finite, `borderRadius` 0 or more and finite. Measured against Rapier 0.21 as a black box (`tests/next-revolved.test.js`): the mass, centre and principal moments of sharp shapes within 1e-5 of Rapier's; 1,600 random rays hit the same body as Rapier, within 2e-5 m of an exact f64 surface and 1e-4 of its normal, never further off than Rapier; casts within 1e-5 m of the exact first touch, where Rapier's are up to 9.4e-4 m off; a cylinder on its cap and on its side and a cone on its base rest within 0.01 m of where Rapier rests them. A ray from 20 to 400 m away meets a sharp cone within 4 float32 rounding units of its length (`kernel/tests/revolve.rs`). By method: contacts by GJK and the expanding polytope on the core with the border as a margin, plus the cap, side line or apex that faces the other shape; a cap's contact points lie on a 16-sided polygon whose corners are on the rim, inside it by at most 1.9 percent of the radius between corners; mass and inertia are integrals over the rounded profile turned about the axis, so a round shape weighs its border too. | The border is a margin on GJK, so a round shape needs no mesh and no extra rows; faces held as faces let a cylinder stand on its cap with a full manifold. | A round shape weighs more than on Rapier, which weighs only the core (an accepted difference, more correct); rays and casts on a round shape run GJK's ray cast, slower than the closed forms of a sharp one; between a cap polygon's corners a contact sits up to 1.9 percent of the radius inside the true rim. | Give `mass` instead of `density` to match a Rapier scene's weights. |
| Own physics convex decomposition (`@margoengine/physics/decomposition`, 2026-11-08) (LIM-517) | By design: `convexDecomposition(mesh, options)` returns `{shape: 'convexDecomposition', parts, concavity}`, a collider on `/next` and on the root entry; `/next` refuses the raw mesh with an `unsupported` MargoError naming the entry, so the search is never on its path. The method of Wei, Liu, Ling and Su 2022 (CoACD): concavity max(Hb, 0.3 Rv) on the mesh scaled to a largest side of 1, 3,000 samples per unit area, Monte Carlo tree search over 20 axis planes per axis, 500 iterations, 4 cuts ahead, ternary refinement, merging. Enforced: a closed mesh (each directed edge with its reverse, after welding equal positions when needed), whole triangles, indices in range, finite vertices, a volume; `concavity` above 0; whole `maxConvexHulls`, `planes`, `iterations`, `depth` of at least 1; `timeLimit` above 0. By design: the same mesh, options and seed give the same parts bit for bit in every engine (only + - * / and own roots); `timeLimit` (default 60,000 ms) throws `timeout`, never a part-done answer; `maxConvexHulls` (default 64) stops cutting with the least convex parts cut. By method: neighbours merge only over a whole shared cut face, not over part of one; the candidate planes are the three axes, no PCA (the paper's option). Measured against Rapier 0.21's V-HACD at defaults (`scripts/decomposition-vs-rapier.mjs`, one busy run): tube 4 parts to 7, Eq. 10 0.050 to 0.138; cup of five slabs 12 to 12, 0.032 to 0.035; closed cup 12 to 12, 0.025 to 0.052; torus 6 to 11, 0.036 to 0.030 with 8.8 percent hull volume over the mesh to 3.7; knight 15 to 19, 0.044 to 0.052; 1.1 to 17.3 s where Rapier takes 0.1 to 0.3 s. | A cut mesh keeps fine detail a voxel grid loses, and the tree search finds the cuts a greedy one misses (the paper's Fig. 9), so a body gets fewer, closer parts. | 5 to 120 times Rapier's build time on these meshes (a loss, perf history entry 81); the torus has fewer, looser parts than Rapier's at the same threshold; a mesh with holes is refused where V-HACD voxelizes it. | Decompose once at build time and store the parts; lower `iterations` or `planes` for speed; close a mesh's holes first, or give a fixed body a `trimesh`. |
| Own physics body kinds and mass (`@margoengine/physics/next`, preview, 2026-11-07) | By method: a body that stops being fixed finds the fixed colliders its boxes overlap by one walk over the sorted ends, so `setType` costs the world's collider count once; a body made fixed drops its pairs with fixed bodies. Enforced on both entries: `additionalSolverIterations` takes any whole number from 0 to 4294967295, Rapier's 32 bits, and throws a `RangeError` past it, where the root entry used to wrap 2^32 + 3 to 3 passes without a word; the kernel adds the counts without wrapping. | One body, one walk, no index kept for a rare call. | Measured on the root entry and matched: `setType(type, false)` keeps a sleeper asleep until the next step, which wakes it and what it touches whatever `wakeUp` said, and a body made fixed is not woken before that step; a still kinematic body reads asleep after the sleep window, `sleep()` stops it and a target wakes it (`next-bodies.test.js`). Accepted difference: the own solver wakes a whole sleeping island at the call (`setType(type, true)`, a velocity, an impulse), so between the call and the next step the bodies it touches read awake, where Rapier reads them asleep until its step wakes them; every pose and velocity of every step is the same. Accepted difference: a fixed body always reads `sleeping` false here; Rapier keeps a flag on fixed bodies that `sleep()` sets and that changes nothing. By method: a step runs `iterations` plus the most any awake island asks for, and an island takes the last of them, so it costs what a world of that many passes would for its own rows, plus a lookup per pair and path while any island asks. By design: with no collider mass, `setMassProperties` and `setAdditionalMass` figures are the body's exactly; with collider mass, the sum goes through the Jacobi rotations of `mass.rs` and rounds as any inertia does. Seen in the tests: a door motor left to sleep stops when its approach slows under the sleep threshold, 0.005 rad short of 0.6 for stiffness 40; with `canSleep: false` it reaches 0.6 within 3e-6 A sleeping motor is on the list for the speed batch to compare with Rapier. | Give a body its final `type` when you add it instead of calling `setType` often. Set `canSleep: false` on a body a motor drives, so it keeps moving. |
| Own 2D physics solver (`packages/physics2d/kernel`, native only until `@margoengine/physics2d/next`, 2026-11-08) (LIM-527) | Enforced: polygons of up to 8 corners; by default 4 substeps a step, each one biased and one relaxing pass. | Contacts are soft constraints with substeps (Catto, Solver2D, 2024): springs that push an overlap out at no more than 3 m/s, so stacks do not hop, held to a quarter of the substep rate (60 Hz), or an eighth (30 Hz) for an unloaded pair of dynamic bodies at one pass, which lets a deep pile settle and sleep. | By method: a pile sits lower than its boxes add up to, by its load over the springs: a two-point contact carrying load L sinks L g k / (2 omega^2), k the point's inverse effective mass. The 20-row pyramid's top drops 28.26 mm, 28.35 mm when built top down (Box2D v3 29.11, Rapier 2D 29.56 on the same scene), and a heavy box on three light ones at 1:100 drops 52.29 mm, 0.6 percent from the closed form; nothing moves once resting. The contacts are solved colour by colour, coloured from the top of the scene down along gravity, so a pile rests the same whatever order its bodies were made in. The 100-row pyramid's top drops 684 mm by 10 s (Box2D v3 726, Rapier 2D 735) and its row 30 spreads 48 mm (70, 64): a pair keeps last step's reference face while the two faces' separations lie within one slop (5 mm), and a clipped point keeps the id of the end it replaces, so resting points keep their warm start. Positions move in doubles and contacts are worked out relative to a pair's first body, so a pile 10 km out rests as the same pile at the origin. | More substeps or passes stiffen the springs: 8 substeps of 2 passes at `contact_hertz` 120 drop the pyramid's top 2.09 mm. |
| Own 2D physics polylines (`packages/physics2d/kernel`, native only until `@margoengine/physics2d/next`, 2026-11-08) (LIM-529) | Enforced: a chain needs 2 points (3 when closed) after points closer than 2.5 mm to the one before are dropped. | Each segment knows its neighbours' far vertices (ghosts), so a shape crossing a join meets one surface, not the next segment's end edge on (Catto, Ghost Collisions, 2020). | By method: a contact's normal tilted toward a join is kept only at a corner that turns away from the shape, and only up to the neighbour's face; at a flat or concave join the contact is the face's. A box and a ball slide at 5 m/s across 50 joins keeping vx at 5 within 1e-4 (Box2D v3's chain: the same; Rapier 2D's polyline catches, vx down to -0.17). Separate segments or tile boxes still catch at their seams, as they do in both references. | Build floors and tile outlines as one chain; `create_chain` with `closed` for a loop. |
| Own 2D physics joints (`packages/physics2d/kernel`, native only until `@margoengine/physics2d/next`, 2026-11-08) (LIM-534) | Enforced: revolute, prismatic, fixed, rope and spring joints between two bodies; a revolute limit reads the relative angle from -pi to pi, so its limits lie within that range. | Joints are soft constraints on the substeps as contacts are, at `joint_hertz` 60 (held to a quarter of the substep rate) and damping ratio 2, pulling error out at no more than 3 m/s. Runs of joints (a chain, a limb) are solved exactly in one sweep (Baraff 1996); joints at a body with three or more, and the joint that would close a ring, one joint at a time. A taut joint stiffens its bodies' turning by its pull (geometric stiffness, Andrews, Teichmann and Kry 2017), taken implicitly, so a heavily loaded chain holds at 4 substeps. | By method: the stiffness damps only the stiff zigzag a heavy load sets up in a chain; it moves a pendulum's period by 0.007 percent. A 50-link chain under a 1000:1 end mass stretches at most 0.55 percent (Box2D v3 29.88 times, Rapier 2D 22.18 times on the same scene); with `joint_geometric_stiffness` off it flies to 69 times. | `joint_geometric_stiffness: false` gives the plain inertia. |
| Stage model viewer (`@margoengine/stage/viewer`, 2026-11-05) | A failed `addViewer` puts back what it changed; it does not freeze the camera while it loads, so a visitor who moves it meanwhile keeps that move. WebGPU only, as gltf-pro. One model per viewer; the model must have geometry. A 'cascaded' floor is a plain box 40 model sizes wide, so its far edge shows as a horizon line against the backdrop; 'contact' has no such line. Lamp, shadow and floor values were picked by eye on one model (flight helmet), not measured against a reference. | One call has to fit any model, so sizes come from `model.bounds` at load. An animated model does not resize them, and the contact shadow is cached (`static`) unless `shadow: {static: false}`. | A model that grows or moves keeps its first shadow and framing; a horizon line with 'cascaded'. | Pass `environment`, `background`, `shadow` and `tonemap` to override, `false` to remove a part. Real-GPU check: `npm run test:gpu --workspace packages/stage`. |
| Headless stage (`@margoengine/headless/stage`, 2026-11-05) | No camera controls and no frame loop: `step(delta)` advances time and runs `onTick` and `engine.onFrame` callbacks. Stages alive at once share one `gpu`. In Node, proved on the `webgpu` binding 0.6.2 on Windows (D3D12 and WARP); a textured glTF does not load there (docs/known-bugs.md). | `installGPU` changes globals, so a second implementation at once would replace the first's. The frame loop belongs to core, so the stage keeps its own list of `onFrame` callbacks. | A callback registered before the stage exists, on an engine made by hand, does not run. | Use `stage.onTick` and `stage.step`. Dispose stages on one `gpu` before using another. |
| Image comparison (`@margoengine/headless/compare`, 2026-11-05) | CPU only. Images must be one size. `decodePNG` ignores colour profiles and gamma, and cuts 16-bit samples to 8. SSIM runs on luma, so a change of hue at equal brightness is seen only by the pixel count. | Tests compare a few images, so the plain code is enough, and it runs anywhere. Both measures run in `compareImages` because each misses what the other sees. | A 4K image takes CPU time on every comparison: both passes run in JavaScript, and this has not been timed. | Compare at the size you render your tests, and tune `threshold`, `maxRatio` and `minSSIM` to your scene. |
| Output formats (`@margoengine/headless/formats`, 2026-11-05) | JPEG is baseline with fixed standard tables, so files are larger than an optimized encoder's. Lossless WebP is a plain encoder (subtract green and three predictors, no colour cache), so files are larger than libwebp's. Lossy WebP needs a browser or worker. EXR is uncompressed. The engine's output is tone-mapped sRGB, so true HDR needs `PostStack`'s `sceneTexture`. | Previews and captures are small and written once, and plain code runs in Node with no codec to install. Every file is decoded by the browser's own decoders in the GPU check. | Bigger files than the best encoders; sizes have not been compared. | Use lossy WebP in a browser for the smallest previews, or run the file through a dedicated optimizer. |
| Picks in a loop (`engine.pick`, 2026-11-06) | By method: one readback a point. Core queues overlapping calls and asks a plugin for one at a time, so no package can merge them, and core stays as it is. `picking.pickMany(points)` is the batch call: one readback for all of them. | Keeps core's order: each pick reads the scene as its turn comes. | Each pick in a loop costs one readback a point. | Use `pickMany` for many points. |
| Frame counts (`engine.stats`, 2026-11-07) | No counted threshold; the counts follow the frame last drawn. | By method: two-node models with two meshes and one shared mesh, in `packages/gltf-pro/tests/stats.test.js` and on the real `webgpu` binding; another camera and a pick, with a pass counting as a package does, in `tests/core-parity-hooks.test.js` and on a real GPU in `scripts/core-parity-hooks-gpu-check.js`. | The counts are those of the frame last drawn: `visible` is core's collected meshes plus every node a glTF model draws, `drawCalls` is core's batches plus every package draw that counts itself. After `engine.render({ camera })` with another camera they read that camera's frame, not the engine camera's, and a pick changes neither. With several views they read the last view. | None known. |
| Pipeline compile tracking (`@margoengine/stats/compile`, 2026-11-06) | Sees compiles made after attach; `trackNext()` before `Engine.create` adds the engine's startup pipelines. Keeps the newest 256 records and hitches by default. | Times are CPU time around each call: browsers compile in another process, so a blocking call can return quickly and the stall lands later. A hitch means a compile ran in a slow frame or the frame before it, which is evidence, not proof of the cause. | Unlabelled compiles show an empty label; the `stacks` option finds them. | Call `trackNext()` before `Engine.create` to include startup pipelines, and use the `stacks` option to find unlabelled compiles. |
| CPU phase breakdown (`@margoengine/stats/phases`, 2026-11-06) | CPU time only, with the clock the browser gives: `performance.now()` steps by 5 to 100 microseconds, so a short phase in one frame can read 0 and the averages are the numbers to trust. The frustum test runs inside the one Wasm kernel call, so it is timed in `tick` and cannot be split from world matrices; `cull` is the visible-instance collection after it. `onFrame` callbacks added before attach fall outside the frame. Other scenes in a multi-view frame have no end pass, so time after their submit reads as `encode`. | The frustum test is inside the kernel's one Wasm call, so it counts in `tick` and `cull` is only the collection after it; splitting them needs a kernel change. | A short phase in one frame can read 0. | Read the averages, which are the numbers to trust. |
| Leak alerts (`@margoengine/stats/leaks`, 2026-11-06) | Needs one full window before it can alert, four minutes by default. Follows 64 numbers by default; labels past that are not followed, the totals are. | Sees what `GPUMemory` sees: objects made after its attach, sized from their descriptors, and a buffer dropped without `destroy()` counts as live. | Growth slower than `minBytes` or `minCount` per window is not reported, and a cycle longer than a quarter of the window can look like growth. | After a device loss the old objects stay counted, so make a new `GPUMemory` and `LeakWatch` for the new device. |
| Field telemetry (`@margoengine/stats/beacon`, 2026-11-06) | Percentiles come from 256 logarithmic bins from 0.1 ms to 10 s, so each reads at most 4.6% above the true value; `max` is exact. Frames under 0.1 ms or over 10 s count in the end bins. A failed send is counted, not retried, and with `sendBeacon` or a `no-cors` fetch the endpoint's reply cannot be read, so `sent` means handed to the browser. A report under `minFrames` when the page closes is lost. `sendBeacon` sends the page's cookies for its endpoint; the `fetch` fallback omits them. Asking users for consent is the app's job. | Percentiles come from 256 logarithmic bins, so they are approximate. | `sent` means handed to the browser, and a report under `minFrames` when the page closes is lost. | Lower `minFrames` (default 60) so a short visit still reports. |
| Quantized glTF on the light path (`@margoengine/gltf/quantized`, 2026-11-06) | By method: integer POSITION and normalized NORMAL turn into floats on load, so the file shrinks on the wire and not on the GPU. Sparse accessors and any other form stay refused. | Core's vertex layout is float xyz, and core is frozen. | A quantized file loads with no gltf-pro, at 24 B per vertex on the GPU. | `@margoengine/gltf-pro` reads the same file. |
| Textured glTF on the light path (`@margoengine/gltf/textured`, 2026-11-06) | By method: base colour map and MASK only, WebGPU only. One extra render pass per frame for all textured meshes of a scene. A cut-out casts its outline in `Shadows` and `VirtualShadows`, from a second copy of its vertices at 80 bytes each. GPU picks (`engine.pick`, `Picking`) see it as solid, since core's pick draws the batch with no uv and no package can change that draw; `AlphaRaycaster` with `texturedAlphaMasks` sees through it on the CPU. Edges are not antialiased inside a texel. `engine.stats.drawCalls` counts these draws. | Core draws one constant colour, so the entry claims the batch and draws it with core's shader plus a texture read. | A one-texture prop loads with no gltf-pro. | `@margoengine/gltf-pro` for other maps, BLEND, compressed textures. |
| Meshopt glTF on the light path (`@margoengine/gltf/meshopt`, 2026-11-06) | Enforced: decoded views may reach 64 MiB per file, checked before each decode, and a stream shorter than its bufferView is a RangeError. Decoding runs on the calling thread. | The light path has no worker, and the decoder is synchronous. | A small compressed file loads with no gltf-pro, and the decoder is fetched only for files that use it. | `@margoengine/gltf-pro/worker` parses in a worker. |
| Instanced glTF on the light path (`@margoengine/gltf/instancing`, 2026-11-06) | Enforced: every instance counts toward `maxNodes` (default 100,000), checked before its nodes are made. By method: an instance costs one core mesh plus one per primitive, and TRANSLATION, ROTATION and SCALE are read, other instance attributes ignored. | Core batches every mesh of one geometry into one instanced draw, so each instance is a node and the file needs no draw path of its own. | Instances move, pick and dispose like any node; the price is memory per instance. | `@margoengine/gltf-pro` keeps instances as rows of one GPU buffer (`PBRInstances`) for scenes with far more copies than nodes. |
| One-call glTF loading (`loadAuto` in `@margoengine/gltf/auto`, 2026-11-06) | By method: moves to `@margoengine/gltf-pro` on an `unsupported` refusal, which includes the limits only the light loader has (one million vertices in total, one million values in an accessor, instances past `maxNodes`). A file over a limit both loaders share (64 MiB, more real nodes than `maxNodes`) rejects with that limit. The light attempt may reach the point of refusal, so a refused file costs that parse as well; each URL is still downloaded once. | The move is keyed on the `unsupported` code alone, so a shared limit error reaches the caller and names its fix. | No guessing which loader to pick; a plain file loads no gltf-pro code. | Raise `maxNodes`, or call gltf-pro directly with `maxDownloadBytes` for a file over 64 MiB. |
| glTF cameras and lights as data (`@margoengine/gltf/scene-data`, 2026-11-06) | By method: values only, nothing is created in the engine; `position`, `direction` and `matrix` are as loaded and do not follow later moves (the `node` handle does). A file with only cameras and lights, and no mesh, is refused. `sceneData` holds nothing for a model gltf-pro loaded. | Core has no scene camera or light object to fill, and which camera wins is the app's choice. | A scene file sets its own view and sun on the light path with no gltf-pro. | gltf-pro's `viewGLTFCamera` and `applyGLTFLights` for a model it loaded. |
| Gaussian splats in glTF (`@margoengine/gltf-pro/splats`, 2026-11-06) | Enforced: 3,000,000 splats a cloud (more start another, and two clouds do not sort against each other), and `maxDownloadBytes` as for any glTF, so a large capture needs it raised. | `@margoengine/splats` takes world-space data and has no transform of its own; Float32 covariances cannot hold a flatter splat. Captures and meshes come from one file through one loader. | By method: a cloud is read once, in the world its nodes had at load, so a later move of a node or of `model.root`, or a `KHR_node_visibility` change, does not reach it. Animated nodes do not move it. A node hidden at load gives no cloud. Splats blend in the renderer's target like every cloud of `@margoengine/splats`, so a tone mapper that runs after it also touches them, where the extension calls their colour display-referred. SPZ is the only compression read, and it is a draft: the file format may change. A splat flatter than 1/1024 of its longest axis is drawn at that ratio. | Raise `maxDownloadBytes` for a large capture. |
| UV sets in glTF (`@margoengine/gltf-pro`, 2026-11-06) | Enforced: `TEXCOORD_0` to `TEXCOORD_3`; a fifth set is refused by name. By method: sets 2 and 3 live in a second vertex buffer, 16 bytes a vertex, only on primitives whose material or variant reads them. Those stay out of static batches, a `MASK` or `BLEND` base map on set 2 or 3 casts and picks uncut, a `.margo` file holds two sets, and `writeGeometry` needs `uvs` to change the vertex count. | The 80-byte vertex and everything that reads it stay as they were, and a file that only carries the sets pays nothing. | Shadows and picks of a cut-out on a third or fourth UV map show the whole shape. | Put a cut-out's base map on set 0 or 1. |
| Volume scatter in glTF (`KHR_materials_volume_scatter` in `@margoengine/gltf-pro/extended` and `install` of `@margoengine/materials`, 2026-11-06) | By method: follows the draft of Khronos pull request 2453 as of 2026-11-06, so the extension can still change, and pull request 2579 proposes another in its place. | A rasterizer has no path through the medium; the lobe is what a dense medium converges to by the spec's own comparison. No light is walked through the medium: the scattered share comes back at the surface as one diffuse lobe, so there is no sideways spread, thin edges do not glow from light hitting the far side beyond what diffuse transmission gives, and anisotropy only scales the amount (1 - g), with no phase function drawn. Forward scattering adds nothing back to the straight view through specular transmission, whose Beer-Lambert term keeps the whole extinction. The depth is the authored thickness along the normal, not the mesh's real depth. The map slots count toward the eight extension textures of an extended material. Through `install` the lobe takes the sun and the hemisphere only, as transmission there has no lighting effects; node materials and `Material` do not read it. | Files with skin or wax that need a soft glow look harder than in a path tracer. | Add `KHR_materials_diffuse_transmission`, which the same entry reads, for light passing through thin parts. |
| Texture mip streaming (`textures` of `@margoengine/gltf-pro/progressive`, 2026-11-06) | Enforced: only KTX2 streams; PNG, JPEG, WebP and AVIF hold no mips and load whole, and a KTX2 file stored with one level gains nothing. A stream keeps its coarsest levels whatever the budget, and the budget is per load, not shared between models. By method: size on screen comes from each primitive's bind-pose bounds and UV density from up to 4,096 of its triangles, so a skinned or morphed primitive counts as its rest pose, a hidden node counts as seen, and every view counts at the canvas height. Off WebGPU the option has no effect; `uploadImage` and a replica on another device refuse a streamed image. Streamed bytes do not count toward `maxDownloadBytes`; `budgetBytes` bounds them on the GPU. After a device restore a slot draws its default until the file opens again. | Only KTX2 holds mips, so PNG, JPEG, WebP and AVIF load whole. | Measured on the real Khronos FlightHelmet (glTF-KTX-BasisU, CC0, 17 files, 33,959,955 B): 4,163,257 B read and 2,604 B of texture on the GPU with the camera 40 units away, of which 3,227,148 B is the geometry; 12,977,842 B and 76,808,172 B close up. Timing not measured: needs a quiet machine and a real network. | A scene whose textures are PNG or JPEG gains nothing; convert them to KTX2 with mips. |
| Fixture and image lights in glTF (`EXT_lights_ies` and `EXT_lights_image_based` in `@margoengine/gltf-pro/lights`, 2026-11-06) | By method: follows the extensions' READMEs on the Khronos glTF repository as of 2026-11-06 (`EXT_lights_ies` Vendor, status Complete; `EXT_lights_image_based` Multi-Vendor); neither is a draft. Enforced: at most 16 fixtures that differ in profile or aim (the 16 profiles of `IESLights`), because each aim is its own resampled profile on the 1 by 5 degree grid. Type A photometry is refused, which the extension allows. | Both lights go through the clustered and environment paths already there, so the entry adds no shader. | Lights are placed once, where their nodes are when `applyGLTFLightExtensions` runs: they do not follow later node moves, animation or visibility. Type B axes are checked on a real report (GE Evolve LED Flood, MIT, in the repository): every V plane reads 19.5905 cd at H = 90, so the planes meet there, and the table holds 5,871.8 lm with H as the latitude against 5,871 stated (6,531.1 the other way round), 5,874.6 lm through the parsed grid. By method: the file cannot show the sign of V, so which way is up comes from the extension's frame; LM-75 was not available. `TILT` data is not applied, which the extension allows. In `EXT_lights_image_based` the file's lower specular mips are not read, because `Environment` prefilters from mip 0, and `irradianceCoefficients` is returned as data and not used: the extension does not state their scale, and on the one real file no reading tried matched them (their first row within 21 percent, the rest about twice off). The sRGB decode of rgb / alpha is read from that same file too, since the extension names no transfer. A file from `loadGLTFProgressive` or the stream loader has no base URL, so relative files need `baseURL`, and its `bufferView` files are not there until its buffers arrive. | A file whose fixtures move, or that aims more than 16 ways, needs its lights set by hand through `IESLights`. |
| Behaviour graphs in glTF (`KHR_interactivity` in `@margoengine/gltf-pro/interactivity`, `@margoengine/animation/interactivity`, 2026-11-06) | Measured: 1,155 of 1,156 conformance sub-tests, 187 of 187 invalid graphs rejected. The failure is `/scene isValid` in `pointer/CoreReadOnlyPointers_GetTests`: the suite declares `/scene` an `int` and expects 0, the glTF Object Model table says `ref`, and Margo keeps the specification. Enforced: 1,000,000 node activations a step (`maxActivations`), past which the graph halts for good; 10,000 custom events a tick (`maxEventsPerTick`), the rest waiting; 65,536 active delays, moves or playing clips, past which an operation takes its `err` flow. By method: `custom` typed values are opaque; time moves once a frame, so a delay fires on the first frame at or after its time; the first tick is at time zero. Hover stops at the first object, as it uses one GPU pick; a click passes through unselectable nodes of the model, not through other models. A click tests both faces of each triangle on the CPU, once per click. Materials the live writers do not drive (`behaviour.notes` lists them) hold their value for the graph and change nothing on screen. `ref` is missing from the Validation section's signature list, which is read as an erratum. | The specification's limits on a Turing-complete graph are left to the runtime, and a file is untrusted. | A graph past the limits halts or has the operation take its `err` flow, and materials the live writers do not drive change nothing on screen. | Raise the limits in the options; for the `/scene` sub-test, the suite and the Object Model have to agree first. |
| Author, licence and copyright in glTF (`KHR_xmp_json_ld` in `@margoengine/gltf-pro/metadata`, 2026-11-06) | By method: follows the ratified extension, Khronos main at 736150dc. Read only: nothing in the repository writes the extension yet, so `@margoengine/exporters` drops packets on export, and the merging the extension describes is left to the caller. Metadata sits on the seven object kinds the extension lists; a packet another extension references, as the `KHR_audio_emitter` sound of a real file does, is not tied to that object and reads through `packets`. XMP embedded in a referenced PNG or JPEG is not read, which the extension allows. Enforced: values nested past 64 levels are refused. | XMP has one free-text rights field, and real files put a copyright line or a licence name in it; the 64 levels bound a recursive walk over file input. | `license` and `copyright` both fall back to `dc:rights`, so the two can show the same text; `rights` is always the value as written. A bad packet list, `packet` reference or `@context` throws a `TypeError` from `readGLTFMetadata`, and the model still loads and draws. | `packets[i].raw` holds each packet as written, and `packets[i].get(namespace, name)` reads any other property. |

Enforced: 15 distinct collision filters, as a Rapier world has 16 interaction groups and colliders with no filter take one. By method, where Rapier differs from the draft: the rule that wins between two materials is max, multiply, min, average (the draft has average, minimum, maximum, multiply); friction has one coefficient, the dynamic value; a limit with stiffness is held hard; a range on two or three axes at once is a box, where the draft has a cylinder, a sphere or a cone, and a lower bound on several axes at once is not held; a finite or double sided plane is a box 2 mm thick, its open sides 1 km; a sphere under uneven scale, a cylinder with two radii and a capsule with two radii are convex hulls of 194, 64 and 388 points. A mesh collider is the mesh at rest: skins and morph weights do not deform it. Not followed: animated or written object model properties beyond joint drives and a motion's velocities and gravity factor. | A draft has no conformance tests, and each of these is what Rapier offers. | `world.notes` names the node and what differs, except the combine rule, which only shows when two materials disagree. Past 15 filters `applyGLTFPhysics` throws a `RangeError`. | Merge filters that act alike in the file; set friction and limits on `world.bodies` and `world.joints` by hand. |
| Bump maps in glTF (`EXT_materials_bump` in `@margoengine/gltf-pro/extended`, 2026-11-06) | One height map per material, and it counts toward the eight extension textures of an extended material: a ninth texture gets no bump. | The gradient comes from screen differences, three map reads per pixel, so it needs no tangents and no extra vertex data. Files with fine detail in a bump map show it, for one extended shader variant and 48 B more per extended material. | By method: follows the draft of Khronos pull request 2339 as of 2026-11-04, so the extension can still change. Without `heightSpace` the draft sets no scale, and Margo reads `bumpFactor` as metres (`world`). `heightSpace: object` takes the scale from the node, so each instance's own scale under `EXT_mesh_gpu_instancing` and a joint's scale in a skin do not count. A material using it stays out of static batches, as a batch has one node. Clearcoat keeps its own normal: the bump tilts the base layer only, as the draft says. | Leave `heightSpace` at its default `world`, where `bumpFactor` is in metres and node, instance and joint scale do not enter. |

Run on the RTX A6000 only. | By method: RGBA8 output only, 4 bytes a texel where BC7 costs 1; ETC1S streams still read on the CPU, on the calling thread. | Use `@margoengine/decoders/texture`, which transcodes with the Wasm module to BC7, ASTC or ETC2 (BC7 costs 1 byte a texel). |
